Activity 2.2: Implement and mainstream new ecosystem-based flood risk reduction and climate change 
adaptation methods. (GCF Financing = $ 5,868,747; Co-Financing = $20,247,428) 
 
98. Nature-based approaches which can provide added benefits such as enhanced ecosystem services will be 
implemented in the highest risk basins in B&H. These solutions will complement structural measures to ensure that

=== PAGE 40 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 36 
 
 
 
38 Aforestation will be conducted using only native species. Ownership of land is within government, therefore it will be closely monitored by 
its representatives. For the grassing, it will be restoration of grasslands that were previously degraded. 
the combination of both structural and ecosystem-based measures provides the best solution to climate-resilient 
flood management and protection. Non-structural measures (See Annex A of FS) will include: floodplain 
reconnection, selective bed raising/riffle creation, wash lands, wetland creation, two-stage channels, re-
meandering straightened rivers, land and soil management activities to retain / delay surface flows, woody debris 
dams on streams and tributaries, flood plain woodland, re-forestation, agro-forestry, creation or re-instatement of 
a ditch network to promote infiltration (swales, interception ditches, etc), in-channel vegetation management growth 
to maximise channel roughness.  
  
99. Under this activity the project will implement 21 non-structural measures which were identified out of 69 structural 
and non-structural measures proposed by WA’s in the course of feasibility analysis (See FS Annex A for list of 
measures and Annex E for description of appraisal and selection process). Of those, 8 non-structural 
measures will be funded by GCF resources, and 13 measures will be co-funded by the Government of B&H. Cost 
benefit analysis was carried out on all 69 measures based on the methodology described in the Economic Analysis 
annex of the proposal and projects were ranked on the basis of the benefit to cost ratio. The two highest priority 
measures from each target basin were selected (except Vrbas where several non-structural measures have already 
been implemented). A summary table of non-structural measures to be implemented by the project is provided in 
Annex A of the FS.  
 
100. 
The project will include community based EbA  as part of the solution, which will be particularly important to 
reduce the impact of torrential floods. The project will scale up successful EbA approaches piloted by the 
UNDP/SCCF Vrbas River project.  The highest priority eco-system-based measures will be implemented in the 
Vrbas basin which is the only basin in B&H with already developed torrential flood hazard maps and prioritized and 
costed EbA measures to reduce torrential flood risk.   In the Vrbas River Basin (and all other basins), one of the 
main sources of sediment production is a network of forest roads and paths for hauling logs. During the war, roads 
were cut in the forest for illegal logging and as a result, most of the deforestation happened then. The likelihood of 
expanding the roads for timber extract now is low, as there are entity legislations which prohibit cutting of such path 
(Federal law on forest, article 13 and 14 and Law on Forest Republika Srpska, article 82). During the construction 
of the road, most of the excavation material is simply pushed from the route down the slope most of which reaches 
the river network and is transported all the way to a recipient, usually the Vrbas main river.  Forest paths for 
extraction of timber are usually designed with high angle of slope (over 50%), which causes significant erosion. 
Density of these forest paths is approx. 150-200m/hA, which sums up length of approx. 21,000 km in basin area. 
This area is exposed to extensive erosion and creates torrential floods which will produce significant amounts of 
sediment. Sediment in watercourses increases the risk of floods, and rapid drainage of water from slopes reduces 
concentration time.  It is necessary to take measures to prevent erosion on forest roads and paths and to rehabilitate 
areas already affected.  In the Josavka River sub-basin of the Vrbas Basin the following EbA flood risk management 
measures will be implemented: 9 partitions, 16 wicker fields, afforestation of the basin in the amount of 200,00 ha 
and grassing of the areas of approximately 80,00 ha38. Detailed design documentation will be developed for all 
elements but outline design drawings are provided in Annex A of the FS. 
 
101. 
Gender mainstreaming Activities:  Review of the community based EbA and EbA approaches piloted by the 
UNDP/SCCF Vrbas River project by gender expert to identify gender gaps, locate mainstreaming opportunities and 
uncover specific ways in which flood risk and climate change impact women, taking into account gender specific 
vulnerabilities and intersectional issues (single parenthood, age, income levels, disability, etc.); Mainstreaming of 
gender throughout all the new ecosystem-based flood risk reduction and climate change adaptation methods being 
implemented (e.g., afforestation, agroforestry), the project will conduct an assessment to ensure adequate 
opportunities for women to equally benefit from the work, employment and income making opportunities associated 
with implementing these measures. Gender mainstreaming into EbA design methods will make following 
considerations:  
• 
Participation and Representation: Engage and involve women, men, and diverse social groups in the 
design and decision-making processes of EbA initiatives. Ensure their meaningful participation, 
representation, and inclusion in planning, implementation, and evaluation stages. Consider local 
knowledge, priorities, and perspectives, and involve community leaders, women's groups, and 
marginalized populations. 
• 
Gender-Sensitive Vulnerability and Capacity Assessments: Conduct gender-sensitive vulnerability and 
capacity assessments to understand the specific needs, roles, and experiences of women, men, and 
different social groups in relation to flood risk and ecosystem services. This will help to identify their specific

=== PAGE 41 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 37 
 
 
 
39 For example:  Space for the Rivers Policy – Netherlands; Making space for Water Policy in England; LIFE projects (Donau-Auen and 
DonauUfer) along the Danube downstream from Vienna,  
vulnerabilities, capacities, and priorities, and inform the detailed design of EbA interventions that address 
their unique circumstances. 
• 
Access and Benefit-Sharing: Ensure equitable access and benefit-sharing of ecosystem services and 
resources. Recognize and address potential gender and social disparities in access to and control over 
natural resources, such as land, water, and forests, that are essential for implementing EbA measures. 
Promote inclusive mechanisms for sharing benefits derived from EbA initiatives, considering the needs and 
rights of different groups. 
• 
Knowledge and Capacity Building: Provide training, knowledge-sharing, and capacity-building initiatives to 
enhance the understanding and skills of community members, particularly women and marginalized 
groups, in relation to EbA and flood risk management. This empowers individuals to actively participate in 
decision-making, contribute their knowledge, and take leadership roles in the implementation and 
maintenance of EbA measures. 
• 
Gender-Responsive Communication: Ensure gender-responsive communication and information sharing 
throughout the design and implementation of EbA initiatives. Use inclusive communication strategies and 
diverse channels to reach different groups, considering language preferences, literacy levels, and 
accessibility requirements. Tailor messages to address specific gender and social norms, roles, and 
interests. 
• 
Safety and Accessibility: Address safety and accessibility concerns in the design and implementation of 
EbA interventions. Consider the specific needs of women, children, elderly individuals, and people with 
disabilities, ensuring that the infrastructure and access routes associated with EbA measures are safe, 
inclusive, and accessible to all 
• 
Monitoring and Evaluation: Incorporate gender and social inclusion indicators into the monitoring and 
evaluation frameworks of EbA initiatives. Regularly assess the social, economic, and environmental 
impacts of the interventions on different groups, including their implications for gender equality and social 
equity. Engage with communities to gather feedback and ensure that the benefits and risks associated with 
EbA are distributed fairly. 
 
102. 
These considerations will be integrated into the design of ecosystem-based approaches for flood risk 
management. For the sites to be implemented with EBA solutions the above considerations will be ensured through 
the ESIA and ESMP and the ESMP monitoring plan.  This will ensure that EbA interventions address the diverse 
needs and priorities of communities, promote social equity, and contribute to long-term resilience and sustainable 
development.

capacity development plan. Under this activity the immediate priorities of the capacity development and training 
plans will be implemented. The list of proposed training and capacity building courses is presented in the Feasibility 
Study (Annex 2). 
 
108. 
The project will also assist the government of B&H in shifting from ad-hoc project-based awareness and 
education efforts to a planned, consistent and sustainable awareness raising and education for enhanced flood

Output 3:  Climate-proof flood protection measures scaled-up through new and improved national and local 
investment frameworks increasing resilience of the most vulnerable groups to climate induced flooding 
Activity 3.1: Develop a country-wide investment framework for climate induced flood risk reduction and 
management including provisions for private sector engagement in climate risk financing. (GCF Financing = 
$480,440; Co-Financing = $603,174) 
 
111. 
Climate risk financing and risk transfer in B&H is currently largely undertaken by the public sector with 
support from the international donor community and lacks the financing mechanisms and tools that would enable 
long-term comprehensive and consistent financing and implementation of risk management and risk reduction and 
financial resilience building measures. Willingness to pay (WTP) surveys undertaken during feasibility study found 
that, for a number of public and private sectors and different types of beneficiaries, there was willingness to 
contribute to risk financing and risk transfer, to ensure the effective long-term management of flood risk and risk 
reduction and to build financial resilience to flooding. The survey identified key private sector players with an  
interest in sector-specific climate risk information products, and their willingness to pay for tailored products that 
will be used in their operations, and their willingness to support or partially support the O&M of hydrometric 
monitoring and early warning systems, equipment and information products has been assessed and determined 
through consultation during proposal development. The WTP studies also identified key sector players with a desire 
for  natural disasters’ insurance as an adequate alternative to compensation for the damage in terms of their own 
resources, credits and donations (Annex H of FS). Significant aspect defining the demand of businesses for  natural 
disasters’ insurance have been identified.  
 
112. 
  The project will develop the Investment framework for climate induced floods risk reduction and management 
including provisions for public and private/productive sector engagement in climate risk financing and risk transfer 
mechanisms.  No GCF proceeds will be used to provide insurance. 
 
 
113. 
Under this activity the project will develop risk financing and transfer mechanisms based on detailed socio-
economic risk, damages, and losses assessment.  The work will be largely co-financed by the partners from 
national insurance sector. This activity will include: 
• 
The identification or public-sector risk financing mechanisms for flood risk management;  
• 
Identification and development of risk financing and transfer mechanisms products and tools, based on detailed 
socio-economic risk, damages and losses assessment;  
• 
Development of natural disasters’ insurance products for public institutions, businesses and agriculture   
• 
Technical advice/training to financial institutions and financiers, including micro-financing institutions, for the 
development of lending products to mainstream the use of climate-risk information into their lending

=== PAGE 44 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 40 
 
 
processes.  that maximises performance and minimises risks and to provide loans to climate 
responsive/resilient enterprises in B&H  (i.e., loans that offer better conditions than the current market 
practices to borrowers - the conditions are interest rate, loan period and easier credit criteria).  These loans 
will service Small and Medium Enterprises and will provide loans for start-up or expanding climate resilient 
enterprises, or climate proofing the enterprise.  
 
114. 
With regard to public sector risk financing, the project will build upon municipal level climate risk-informed 
budgeting mechanisms developed under NAP project and examine opportunities to introduce climate risk-informed 
budgeting into B&H’s existing public financial management (PFM) framework leveraging on existing strengths and 
addressing any limitations of the existing budgeting processes that currently hinder climate risk-informed budgeting 
for flood risk management. This will include using the damage and loss and risk modelling tools (Sub-activity 1.2) 
to provide the evidence basis of existing cost of disasters to enable allocation of funding to different activities within 
the FRM framework and the investment required, FRM/disaster budget ring-fencing mechanisms (such as 
introduction of specific budget codes), introduction of value for money (VfM) analysis and reporting based on cost-
benefit analysis and data to demonstrate the advantages (and higher VfM) of greater spending on FRM, 
preparedness and mitigation. Similarly, mechanisms to monitor whether funds indeed achieved their intended 
purpose through monitoring to enable performance-based-budgeting, integrating FRM performance objectives and 
establishing a  reporting system that enables tracking disaster related expenditure on an annual basis (e.g. as part 
of the Spending Review process). Such evaluations of key programmes will generate key evidence which should 
feed back into the budget allocation process.  
 
115. 
The project will develop and implement a programme of engagement of relevant state institutions, insurers and 
reinsurers, financial institutions, capital market institutions, forming key public-private sector partnerships to 
leverage and scale up investments. The project is also making interventions at the legislative and governance 
levels and strengthening the financial institutions to provide climate responsive financing options and products 
within the existing markets, which will ensure long-term sustainability of the mobilized investments, with the 
opportunity for future scaling up. The project will provide technical advice and training to financial institutions and 
financiers, including micro-financing institutions, for the development of lending products and to enable them to 
mainstream the use of climate-risk information into their lending processes that maximizes performance and 
minimizes risks and to provide loans to climate responsive/resilient enterprises in B&H  (i.e., loans that offer better 
conditions than the current market practices to borrowers - the conditions are interest rate, loan period and easier 
credit criteria).  These loans will service Small and Medium Enterprises and will provide loans for start-up or 
expanding climate resilient enterprises, or climate proofing the enterprise.   
 
116. 
A key risk transfer mechanism which will be supported by the project is natural disaster risk insurance.  The 
project will further develop an enabling regulatory environment and products for natural disasters insurance for 
individual households that were initiated in Vrbas project as well as  explore opportunities and identify other 
products and tools. The Vrbas project identified and developed the type of scheme that would be most appropriate 
for B&H and considered ex-ante, ex-post, index-based, and indemnity-based schemes, with various configurations 
of the roles of both private insurance sector and government. Please refer to the Feasibility Study (Chapter 10 and 
Chapter 21, Annex H) for the detailed recommendations. In addition, the draft Terms and conditions and tariff for 
mandatory property insurance from damages caused by natural disasters, was developed. Initial consultation with 
key stakeholders (community, municipality, private sector companies, insurance companies) on the proposed flood 
insurance scheme and the insurance zone designations revealed general acceptance of the proposed scheme 
(see Annex 7 for stakeholder consultations and Annex 2, Feasibility Study for the results of the WTP survey). The 
Ministry of Finance has been actively engaged in the consultations. Also, the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and 
Water Management is committed to develop a flood insurance framework within EU Flood directive, which was 
included into the Law on Waters in the RS. Moreover, private sector insurance companies as well as reinsurance 
companies are providing 75% co financing to support this activity. The project will provide technical advice to 
identify potential sector-specific natural disaster (including floods) insurance product packages for each of the 
defined sectors, and provide technical advice and training of insurance sector practitioners in the development of 
insurance products packages from natural disasters adjusted to the needs of the assessed sectors and insurers. 
The project will support the introduction of micro-insurance for agricultural producers, entrepreneurs and small and 
medium-sized enterprises in different industries based on feasibility of connecting micro-insurance and 
microfinance. The micro-insurance concept is largely unknown in B&H, while the microfinance sector, is quite well-
developed and regulated, which is often prerequisite for the successful launch of microinsurance in a new market.

=== PAGE 45 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 41 
 
 
Hence the project will leverage and enhance existing micro-finance capacity in B&H to extend to include micro-
insurance.  In order for the micro-insurance program to be implemented in practice, the project will facilitate changes 
to the current regulations in the field of insurance.  In addition, the project will develop three feasibility studies which 
include, a) micro-insurance for agricultural producers, entrepreneurs, and small and medium-sized enterprises in 
different industries; b) regional disaster risk insurance program to increase the degree of diversification of risk; c) 
catastrophe bonds to transfer disaster risk to the capital market.  
   
117. 
By the end of the project the following will be achieved: public sector risk financing and budgeting mechanisms 
developed, public-private sector partnerships established, and  public-private sector climate risk financing 
mechanisms and tools developed,  natural disasters insurance model for public institutions; natural disasters 
insurance model for agriculture, natural disasters insurance model for businesses.  
 
118. 
As part of the investment framework, under this activity the project will also develop a methodology and a tool 
for appraisal-led design for structural and non-structural climate-informed FRM measures, FRM investment 
planning and decision-making, based on cost benefit analysis (CBA). The project will scale up the bespoke GIS-
based socio-economic risk model that was developed for the Vrbas project to provide a country-wide tool for flood 
socio-economic risk modelling, CBA and the identification and appraisal of climate resilient intervention measures. 
The tool will support strategic planning, development of risk financing, transfer and management strategies and 
investment planning. The GIS-based tool will be extended to include an additional module to enable identification 
of flood risk financing requirements for anticipatory, short, medium, and long-term measures, and strategies from 
which annual investment plans can be developed.   A key element of FbF is the allocation of financial resources in 
advance for the anticipatory response to disasters, based on specific forecast threshold that triggers the release of 
those resources for the implementation of early actions based on an Early Action Plan (EAP).  Under this output 
the project will develop mechanisms and tools for ensuring financing for early action interventions to be included in 
the EAP and the municipal investment plans (See Activity 3.2 below).  FRM practitioners will be trained in the use 
of the appraisal and financing methods and models and importantly, capacity will be built to enable the use of 
updating and maintenance of the models. Municipal planners and managers will also be trained in the use of the 
models for appraisal led FRM planning. 
 
119. 
Hence under this activity the project is developing innovative financing options across risk financing and risk 
transfer, enabling blended finance (state funds, loans, micro-finance, insurance, micro-insurance, regional 
insurance pool, catastrophe bonds), forming key public-private sector partnerships to leverage and scale up 
investments, and is making interventions at the legislative and governance levels and strengthening the financial 
institutions to provide climate responsive financing options. This proposal therefore represents a good example of 
finance blending and innovative adaptation finance.    
 
120. 
Gender mainstreaming Activities:  Representatives of different genders and socially excluded groups will be 
included in the teams for development of a national investment framework for climate induced floods risk reduction 
and management; Development of a gender responsive strategy and gender sensitive tools for private sector 
engagement in long-term climate-proof risk management. Development of risk financing and transfer mechanisms 
based on detailed gender sensitive assessment on socio-economic risk, damages, and losses.

Activity 3.2: Formulate multi-year climate resilient municipal investment plans and gender responsive 
community preparedness plans in selected municipalities and in one canton in Vrbas, Una-Sana, Bosna, Drina, 
Neretva and Trebišnjica basins. (GCF Financing = $ 525,000; Co-Financing = $ 3,097,884) 
 
 
122. 
The project will develop municipal multi-year investment plans for climate resilient FRM planning for 10-12 
highest risk communities and for one canton.  It will develop new approaches to investment planning to include: 
annual and periodic maintenance of FRM interventions; identification of financing models for investment 
maintenance costs (e.g. of community-based scheme that involve the use of tariffs or in-kind contributions to 
establish municipal maintenance programmes or engagement of private sector in infrastructure maintenance 
financing);  event-based forecasting of financing that will be required to respond to disaster events (FbF) and 
development of municipal FRM investment plans based on risk-informed project designs, including maintenance, 
and CBA..  For FbF, the investment plans will define the financial resources that will need to be made available in 
advance of flood events, based on the specific forecast threshold (See Activity 1.3) that would trigger the release 
of those resources for the implementation of early actions. The Early Action Plan (EAP) will be part of the 
preparedness response plan, and will outline the roles and responsibilities of those involved in implementing the 
early actions. The project will work with the IFRC to develop the FbF components of the municipal investment plans 
and the EAP. 
 
123. 
The project will support embedding the use of municipal investment plans in the technical justification for budget 
allocation for FRM activities; identifying and prioritizing flood risk financing, based on the principles of portfolio risk 
assessment (PRA) and associated cost-benefit analysis. Furthermore, the CBA tools to be developed by the project 
will be embedded in municipality as a standardized requirement for developing annual infrastructure investment 
plans as well as Forecast based Financing. As torrents management comes directly under municipal jurisdiction, 
these infrastructure investment plans will include detail design for torrents management. 
 
 
124. 
Using the example of Community Emergency Flood Preparedness plans (CEFP) the project will also support 
the development of community preparedness plans for the most vulnerable 10-12 communities in target basins, 
(excluding Vrbas) in line with the procedure, methodology and content of CEFP in B&H as defined by Law of 
Protection and Rescue in RS and B&H including cantons. According to the Law, the CEFP will consist of the 
following documents: Vulnerability and risk assessment, Prevention plan, Preparedness plan, Mobilization plan, 
Emergency plan, Evacuation plan. 
 
125. 
Gender mainstreaming Activities:  Both, men and women are adequately represented in municipal 
investment planning process; Men and women actively participate in formulation of community preparedness plans 
in selected municipalities; Needs of women and other vulnerable (elderly, disabled, children, Roma) groups are 
incorporated in developed plans.

Activity 3.3: Implement climate-proof structural flood risk reduction and anti-erosion interventions in Vrbas, 
Una-Sana, Bosna and Drina River basins. (GCF Financing = $875,280; Co-Financing = $ 20,939,153) 
 
127. 
This activity will focus on implementation of priority structural flood risk reduction interventions for target basins 
at highest risk. The structural measures will be co-financed through EIB loan resources and Water Agencies’ 
budgets. GCF funds will provide technical assistance to develop climate proofed designs based on new climate 
risk information and design methods. The project will embed climate-change responsive design of flood defenses 
and will develop standard methodologies, tools, guidelines and capacity for climate resilient flood defense design 
and implementation. It will utilize flood hazard and risk modelling and will embed CBA and appraisal-led 
prioritization methods in developing and implementing structural measures that maximize benefit, make allowance 
for climate change and which consider environmental impacts. 10 structural measures have been identified by 
Water Agencies, for implementation (See Annex A of FS).  The risk reduction interventions will be implemented in 
high-risk river basin, which have been prioritized during the project design phase as detailed in Section 2.6 of the 
feasibility study. Based on flood socio-economic risk assessment and identification of the highest risk river basins, 
the target sites are located in Vrbas, Una-Sana and Bosna, Neretva and Trebisnjica and Drina River basins.  
 
128. 
Gender mainstreaming Activities: Both, women and men, and other vulnerable groups (elderly, disabled, 
children, Roma) will be included in processes of stakeholders’ consultation related to implementation of climate-
proof flood risk measures through the site-specific ESIA and ESMPs to be conducted for each structural measure.  
The Project will ensure that specific ways in which flood risk and erosion impact women and men are analyzed and 
considered, and that women and vulnerable group members can equally benefit from technical assistance ensured 
with GCF funds, to develop climate proofed designs based on new climate risk information and design methods. 
The Project will assess and make provisions for women to equally benefit from the work, employment and income 
making opportunities associated with implementing these measures.  
 
129. 
The following sub-activities will be implemented: 
Sub-Activity 3.3.1: Finalize detailed design of climate resilient flood protection structural measures;  
Sub-Activity 3.3.2: Implement new flood defences and the rehabilitation and upgrade of existing flood defences 
with climate proofing.  
Activity 3.3 Deliverables: 
• 
Detailed design of 10 climate-proofed structural measures; 
• 
10 EiB-funded climate-proofed structural measures implemented. 
 
COVID-19 Project co-benefits and opportunities 
 
130. The current COVID-19 global pandemic has highlighted the need for a more holistic approach to hazard 
identification and risk management when faced with systemic risks which are complex, interlinked and non-linear. 
According to UNDRR, with reference to COVID-19, there is an urgent need to change our perception of risks and 
their governance mechanisms, and apply a multisectoral and interdisciplinary approach as these mechanisms

=== PAGE 48 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 44 
 
 
 
41 Raúl Salazar, Chief of the United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR) – Regional Office for the Americas and the 
Caribbean.  
https://www.undrr.org/news/understanding-complexity-risk-post-covid-19-challenge 
 
become more urgent when confronted with climate-related hazards during a pandemic crisis41.  This in line with 
the approach that is being proposed for this project to address FRM in B&H. Hence, to ensure that the project 
meets the additional challenges and opportunities presented by COVID-19, the following table is list of 
opportunities and co-benefits of the project in addressing some aspects of the impacts of COVID-19 on 
communities of B&H.  A risk management matrix will be developed for the project and will include close monitoring 
of the risks and opportunities that COVID-19 will present to the project.   
 
Output/Activity 
COVID-19 co-benefits/opportunities 
Output 1: Climate-informed impact-based FFEWS and an increased generation and use of climate data to reduce 
vulnerability to flood related disasters 
Activity 1.1:  Upgrade and expand the coverage of 
the hydrometric network for enhanced monitoring of 
climate variables in Category I catchments and 
torrential streams 
Expansion of the hydrometric observation network will underly the 
impact based FFEWS which will provide timely information about 
occurrence of flood which will enable people to take early measures 
to minimise flood impact as well as measures to secure drinkable 
water, food and medicine, thus enabling health and sanitation 
conditions and reducing potential exposure to virus in emergency 
centers.  
 
Activity 1.2: Enhanceclimate-induced flood hazard, 
risk and vulnerability information for strategic risk 
management and sound decision making for climate 
induced flood management. 
 
Flood protection of critical infrastructure such as municipal health 
centers and hospitals, to ensure full operational capacity of those 
institutions in the time of pandemic.  The project, through hazard and 
risk modelling and mapping is identifying critical infrastructure by type 
including health facilities that might be at risk from flooding. 
Information collected about the facilities that relates to the capacity to 
treat COVID-19 will also be included in the hazard and risk modelling 
and mapping. 
 
The global COVID pandemic has increased the vulnerability of 
communities in B&H. Under this activity, the project is undertaking 
socio-economic risk and vulnerability modelling and mapping based 
on socio-economic factors derived from socio-economic secondary 
data and primary data from survey methods to be developed by the 
project.  Hence the relevant COVID-19-related vulnerability factors 
can be built into the surveys and reflected in risk modelling and risk 
assessment.  Opportunities will be sought to add a COVID-19 
factor/factors to the risk and vulnerability modelling methodology to 
ensure that the risk assessment methodology reflects COVID 
impacts. 
 
Based on vulnerability surveys, modelling and mapping, the project 
will help identify what additional resources Water agencies and Civil 
protection units may need to allocate to priority areas to protect at risk 
critical infrastructure (including health facilities, utilities like water 
supply, power stations etc), and to prevent cascade impacts of floods 
and spread of COVID-19. New approaches will require more 
resources of financing, human, material than ones before the COVID-
19 era. 
Activity 1.3: Develop an integrated impact-based 
centralized and community-based flood forecasting 
and early warning system (FFEWS).  
 
Proper response to flooding would reduce the spread COVID-19 and 
reduce disaster damage at the same time, thus minimising further 
human losses and economic damage. Response activities that 
include social distancing will be developed as part of the FFEWS and 
response activities, at community level. This will be particularly 
important when developing the Community-based EWS. Community 
engagement will include hygiene promotion with a focus on 
handwashing and social distancing requires full engagement of 
communities. Opportunities will be sought for tailoring warning to 
include any relevant additional COVID-19 relevant warnings.

Investment in new infrastructure will create jobs and deliver tangible 
assets that will fuel long-term economic growth, reducing current 
impact of COVID-19 on local economies. 
 
By reducing impacts of floods on communities and infrastructure, the 
burden on hospitals and emergency services is reduced thereby 
maintaining capacity to deal with COVID pandemic – it is well 
recognised that overloading medical facilities is a key risk associated 
with the pandemic, therefore any activities that help reduce other 
disasters that could overwhelm hospitals are of benefit in managing 
the ongoing COVID threat.

When assessing the vulnerability of communities that will benefit from 
the flood structural measures the project will ensure the vulnerable 
get priority attention during the planning and implementation of 
measures. The elderly and infirm are most at risk to succumbing to 
the virus so consideration will be made to ensure adequate hygienic 
conditions. Low-income communities and informal settlements may 
be particularly vulnerable to spreading because of crowding and 
inadequate services. The project will take full account of such 
vulnerabilities in the site-specific ESPs and implement the necessary 
mitigation measures.   
 
 
B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams) 
131. The project will be implemented by UNDP following UNDP’s Direct Implementation Modality (DIM), according to 
the Standard Basic Assistance Agreement between UNDP and the Government of B&H (SBAA, 1995) and the 
policies and procedures outlined in the UNDP  Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures (POPP) (see 
https://info.undp.org/global/popp/ppm/Pages/Defining-a-Project.aspx). UNDP will assume full responsibility and 
accountability for the overall project management, including monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, 
achievement of project outputs and specified results, the efficient and effective use of resources, and reporting to 
GCF.  
132. The Government of B&H through the GCF NDA requested UNDP to deliver this project through the DIM modality. 
DIM modality is justified given the complexity of B&H’s administrative set-up, that includes two self-governing 
entities and applies multi-layered administrative procedures. The United Nations Development Assistance 
Framework (UNDAF) for 2015–2019 and UNDP’s Country Programme Document (CPD) for 2015 – 2019 for 
Bosnia and Herzegovina, as well as the Draft CPD for Bosnia and Herzegovina (2021-2025) stipulate that all 
projects be implemented under the direct implementation modality (DIM). The DIM will be applied in a way to 
maximize cost-effectiveness and tailored capacity development of counterpart government institutions.  
133. UNDP provides a three – tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at 
regional and headquarters levels.  This includes management of funds, programme quality assurance, fiduciary 
risk management, timely delivery of financial and programme reports to GCF and other requirements as per the 
AMA.The quality assurance role supports the Project Board by carrying out objective and independent project 
oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate project management milestones are managed

actions if required and update the status of these risks by maintaining the project risks log according to the DIM

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
144. The total annual budget for climate adaptation and resilience activities in B&H related to flood risk management 
averages US$40 Million, comprising mainly donor grants (40%) and loans (60%) while the damages due to 
flooding is expected to reach US$ 749.3 Million Expected Annual Damages (EAD) for households, businesses, 
agriculture, and public buildings by mid-century due to climate change. The shortfall in expenditure on climate 
change adaptation is due to a current lack of financial capacity of the B&H Government to fund such activities

=== PAGE 54 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 50 
 
 
 
45 Without the project and in the current situation government spend on FRM is less than the expected EAD and currently there is low 
investment in infrastructure maintenance. Without action and the gap between EAD and FRM financing will widen. Government has committed 
to O&M for 20 years, for all project implemented activities and infrastructure and, in addition, the FRM financing mechanisms we will be 
identifed and established by the project, to ensure longer-term risk financing needs for O&M will be met.   
without assistance45. Bosnia can access loans to undertake structural measures and will take a loan with EIB to 
finance the highest priority structural measures, but it needs technical assistance through timely complementary 
intervention to shift the current paradigm from piecemeal, ex-post facto, project-based efforts to the adaptation 
alternative which will bring long-term transformative change. In so doing the large expected financial burden of 
damages and losses due to the climate-change induced impact of extreme hazardous flood events can be avoided 
in the long-term.  
 
145. The proposed project represents a blended finance package that leverages international climate finance from GCF 
(19.8% of the total project value), EIB loan to the Government of B&H for flood protection measures (29% of the 
total project value), B&H public funds provided by the Entities and municipalities for structural and non-structural 
measures and O&M (42.3% of the total project budget), and the national private sector funds (HPP, insurance 
sector, 7.7% of the total project budget). The GCF grant element has been critical to leverage and consolidate the 
overall project financing package and to secure scaling-up of the investment after the project completion.  The 
Government of B&H and HPP partner is committed to cover O&M costs of equipment and flood defenses installed 
within the project over the 20 years investment lifetime (Annex 13). As part of the GCF-supported intervention, the 
project will develop risk finance instruments and leverage partnerships with the productive sectors to secure inflow 
of private investment into flood risk management, including O&M of FFEWS and flood defenses beyond the GCF-
funded measures. During the project development, UNDP has conducted a series of willingness to pay studies 
which confirmed the willingness of productive sectors (HPPs, agriculture, tourism) to invest in the climate resilient 
FRM provided an adequate enabling environment is established. Thus, the proposed project will catalyze a shift 
in FRM financing in B&H from a purely public sector and donor-dependent funding towards a more elaborate 
financing strategies engaging private sector. GCF technical assistance is required to catalyze such transition.        
 
146. GCF grant resources will provide for the technical assistance and capacity building to ensure that flood risk 
management is undertaken in an integrated manner that secures the lives, livelihoods and assets of the most 
vulnerable people in B&H and reduces their susceptibility to climate -induced flood risk that they have limited 
coping mechanisms to withstand. GCF investment will not result in revenue generation or financial returns to 
private sector, rather GCF will finance public goods and will assist the government of B&H to meet its obligations 
providing flood protection and early warning services to vulnerable communities in the face of climate change. 
Thus, GCF funding will cover additional costs to the Government associated with the increasing climate change 
impacts and risks, including additional monitoring of catchments susceptible to torrential flash flooding, where 
flood risk is increasing, and which is currently missing from the monitoring network. It will also cover the additional 
costs of enhanced climate data and information for effective impact based FFEWS, enhanced capacities of public 
sector to deliver climate information services, enhanced capacities to integrate climate risks into the design and 
implementation of structural and non-structural flood protection measures, and enhanced awareness of climate 
risks and preparedness among vulnerable communities. These project activities are essential for improving 
climate risk information availability to the general public, improving community resilience, and strengthening 
institutions with mandates to strategically manage climate risks. 
 
147. There is currently limited participation of private sector in climate risk resilience building, and risk financing, despite 
the high exposure of the private sector to flood risk (US$ 3,222 billion assets at risk). The project is aiming to 
develop the mechanisms for risk sharing between public and private sector, including development of public-
private collaboration on building climate resilience in critical flood impacted sectors and technical advice to 
financial institutions to mainstream climate change risks into their business models to manage the effects.     
 
148. Through the FRM project on Vrbas basin and other past and on-going initiatives, B&H has made an important 
step toward piloting successful solutions for increased resilience to climate induced floods. However, single-project 
interventions represent only partial solutions that cannot offer the much-needed systematic approach without 
nation-wide scaling up. Currently available funds, public/private or donor driven, are not sufficient to address flood 
risk at the appropriate scale of whole territory of B&H. GCF funding offers the possibility for such systematic 
intervention by enabling different types of measures that in synergy will effectively address flood risk in the main 
river basins in B&H.  
 
Financial Analysis recommendations and conclusions

=== PAGE 55 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 51 
 
 
149. Government investment in flood risk management is mainly focused on ongoing maintenance of very old flood 
defence structures, which are in poor condition as a result of wartime damage, lack of maintenance, and the 
presence of minefields around some facilities (TNC). Funding sources for water management (including FRM) 
activities are defined by the legal regulations in effect in each entity, which set out the water use, the amount of 
charges, beneficiaries and special water charges (See Section 3.6 of FS). Revenues generated for water charges 
are allocated to various priority flood risk management activities. The total annual budget for climate adaptation 
and resilience activities in B&H related to flood risk management averages US$40 Million which currently 
comprises mainly donor grants (40%) and loans (60%).  This is well below the damages due to flooding which is 
expected to reach US$ 749.3 Million Expected Annual Damages (EAD) for households, businesses, agriculture, 
and public buildings by mid-century due to climate change. It should be noted the 2014 flood resulted in $2.7 
Million USD in damages or 15% GDP.  
  
150. Financial analysis of this project’s outputs and activities has been carried out in accordance with the Guidelines 
for the Financial Analysis of Projects of United Nations Development Program. These guidelines clearly mandate 
that a financial analysis of project cash flows be computed, and Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) calculated 
only for those proposed project activities or outputs that can clearly result in direct and quantifiable financial 
revenue generation (incremental earnings from baseline) or a direct and quantifiable financial savings potential to 
the project owners or to the project beneficiaries. These guidelines stand to ensure that GCF’s minimum 
concession policy is always protected in the proposal. 
 
151. This project’s proposed structure includes 4 outputs and 11 activities. However, none of the activities or outputs 
clearly result in direct and quantifiable earnings or direct and  quantifiable savings to the project owners or the 
project beneficiaries.  All the activities and outputs of this proposed project result only in non-attributable savings 
that are of public good in nature to the larger community of the at-risk population in B&H. Hence, a financial 
modelling-based analysis has not been conducted and an FIRR has not been calculated for any of these activities. 
 
152. However, considering the GCF’s minimum concession policy, this project’s proposed activities have been 
analysed from a macro-economic and government perspective to assess the need for GCF grant as the only 
feasible financial instrument to fund the project activities. Hence, taking into consideration factors such as the 
public good nature of benefits arising out of the project, climate change being the key driver, economic, geographic 
and demographic distribution of the target population, impact of COVID-19, B&H’s current economic situation, 
persistent fiscal risks, the World Bank’s observations and the catalytic nature of GCF grants, we recommend the 
following:  
 
• 
GCF funds will leverage co-financing in a form of grants and loan from government and private sectors 
and, at the same time, will ensure additionality in the area of climate changes while simultaneously 
avoiding diversion from development needs.There is significant co-financing from Government of B&H 
and there is no incremental ability to stretch their contributions owing to budgetary and fiscal 
consolidation reasons.  
• 
The nature of the benefits does not accommodate repayment of capital in whatever form or serviceability 
of a loan instrument. 
• 
The nature of the beneficiaries and the level of essentiality of the service do not accommodate repayment 
of capital in whatever form or serviceability of a loan instrument. 
• 
Increase in government spending to address COVID-19 pandemic has strained public finances. 
• 
Private sector willingness to pay will only cover a small percentage of costs for those services directly 
related to private sector interests. 
• 
Hence, it is recommended that in order to reduce / close the existing financing and knowledge gaps and 
barriers to improve resilience of B&H’s population to climate change-induced hazards, catalytic capital 
in the form of GCF grants are essential. 
 
153. GCF grants are critical for this proposed project given the impact of COVID-19, B&H’s current economic situation 
and the persistent fiscal risks. B&H is a transitional economy with 51.8% of the population being rural. Due to the 
war time devastation and a slow transition of the economy, a large part of B&H’s population still lives in poverty. 
The relatively slow rate of the post-war economic recovery has been compounded by the negative impacts of 
climate change on key sectors such as agriculture, energy (hydropower), the environment, in particular, due to 
the increased frequency and magnitude of flood disasters, which have tripled in the last decade.  
 
154. B&H’s economy and its state’s budgets continue to be under stress due to continued tepid growth rates and 
insurgence of COVID-19 pandemic. Due to COVID-19, GDP growth is expected to contract between -3.2% and -

=== PAGE 56 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 52 
 
 
5% from a previously projected GDP growth of 3.4% for 2020 . This is expected due to revenue shortfalls viz-a-
viz higher health, social, and economic-support spending. As per IMF’s Country Note on B&H in response to 
“Request for Purchase Under the Rapid Financing Instrument”, the impact of COVID-19 necessitates a large fiscal 
expansion to increase spending on containment and mitigation of the disease. Consequently, from a previously 
projected surplus of 2% of GDP, the fiscal position in 2020 is now estimated to be a deficit of 4.5% of GDP . The 
sovereign credit rating of B&H is “B with stable outlook” as per S&P and “B3 with stable outlook" as per Moody’s 
which makes B&H fall in speculative credit rating category with high credit risk . In the situation that has brewed 
since COVID-19’s spread, there has been a depreciation of 3% in local currency of B&H. This can be indicative of 
withdrawal of investments from domestic markets and outflow of capital.  
 
155. Before  the COVID pandemic hit the country in 2020, B&H economy growth rate slowed from 3% per annum in 
2016-2018 to 2.7% in 2019. The Industrial recession in the last years is largely related to spillovers from German 
industrial recession in 2018-19,  induced decline of B&H industrial production by 5.5% in 2019. As a consequence, 
B&H reached a deficit of foreign trade in 2019 of 8.0 billion KM, which is a record value since 2008.The EU is the 
target of 73% of B&H exports and B&H is vulnerable to output contraction in EU and CEFTA markets. In addition, 
the fundamental weakness of inward orientation / lack of export orientation with total exports to GDP ratio of 40%, 
B&H lags behind the average for small and open economies in Europe. Relatively low level of development is 
reflected in  GDP per capita at PPP (purchasing power parity) stands at 32% of EU-27 average. Public sector 
output represents particularly large share of B&H economy which in structural terms represents a weakness. 
Private remittances associated with large diaspora and a large number of workers abroad represented around 
8.5% of GDP in recent years. Current account deficit represents 31.69% of GDP in 2019 and is expected to worsen 
due to substantial reduction in remittances in 2020, which the IMF expects at two thirds of 2019 level. 
 
156. According to the ILO, a large part of the workforce is trapped in low productivity jobs in agriculture, construction, 
tourism and retail, which on average command wages that are 10 % lower than the national average. This is a 
signal that the structural transformation of the country has been slow. Private enterprise sector is small (15% of 
working age population works in the formal private sector), it is weakly capitalized and exposed to strong economic 
shocks. Large public sector buffer effectively serves as a shield for about a third of total employees.   
 
157. The COVID-related shock disrupted B&H’s already weak economic growth. The IMF expects real GDP contraction 
of 5% in 2020, which implies GDP loss of 8% compared to the pre-COVID-19 growth trend. After expected 
recovery at a rate of 3.5% in 2021, the GDP is expected to remain around 7% below the pre-crisis trend. B&H 
authorities communicated expected GDP contractions between 3% and 5% in 2020, depending on the scenario 
and entity. However, these forecasts may be overly optimistic as the negative demand shock during summer 
months has been stronger than expected.” 
 
 
158. Due to these economic risks that persist and the World Bank’s observations on the region, B&H is currently not in 
a state to raise external financing in the form of loans to replace GCF’s grant for this proposed project. In addition, 
due to the public good nature of the benefits derived from the project, the serviceability of any such loans is 
uncertain.  The shortfall in expenditure in B&H on climate change adaptation is due to the lack of financial capacity 
of the B&H Government to fund such activities without assistance. The country will take a loan from EIB to finance 
the highest priority structural measures, but it needs technical assistance through timely complementary 
intervention to shift the current paradigm from piecemeal, ex-post facto, project-based efforts to the adaptation 
alternative which will bring long-term transformative change. 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
159. The project’s sustainability and exit strategy is rooted in the key elements of design and implementation. The 
following assures long-term sustainability beyond the project implementation period: 
 
160. 
Investment in human resources and institutions: the project is focused on strengthening institutions that 
will have skilled human resources, information, tools and technologies to effectively pursue their mandate in climate 
risk management (Sub-activity 1.3.4 and Activity 2.4). The project investments will improve availability of climate 
risk information and create effective response mechanisms. This project will incorporate lessons learned and 
benefit from the existing collaborative relationship between UNDP and all the key flood risk management partners 
and will foster strong partnerships with and among relevant institutions to ensure shared goals and shared vision 
beyond the project. The project will help ensure sustainability by supporting all relevant government ministries in 
line with government policy, programme and strategic priorities. The establishment of methods and tools for 
developing and implementing flood risk management strategies, the introduction of risk assessment methods,

=== PAGE 57 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 53 
 
 
standards, tools, guidance and regulatory documents make this project highly replicable within B&H. The 
embedding of risk assessment, risk management and risk reduction methods for flood hazards will be particularly 
important. Furthermore, this project has been developed with full awareness of other climate risk and flood risk 
related projects in the region, which makes it highly complementary, and potentially replicable to other projects 
regionally. As detailed above, this project plans to attract private sector investment – particularly in cost sharing 
regimes for long-term O&M for hydrometric equipment.  Hence, by the end of the project, the activities and 
monitoring network will be self-sustaining into the future. In addition, all activities will be conducted in ways that 
empower local stakeholders. Capacity building programs set up will ensure the ongoing transfer of skills to create 
knowledge sharing and better climate services for the future.  Hence, Sub-Activity 1.3.4: Develop and implement a 
capacity development plan for embedding flood hazard and risk modelling approaches and FFEWS into appropriate 
institutions in B&H, which in particular will conduct FFEWS training on the new system for all institutions involved 
in FFEWS as identified by institutional mapping and institutional arrangement plan for FFEWS, Activity 2.4 which 
will conduct training on proposed FRM policy and sectoral FRM, Sub-Activity 1.4.2 which will develop protocols 
and SOPs on data generation and data management for FRM Work Instructions / Directives / Standard Operating 
Procedures and Sub-Activity 2.1. which will develop country-wide floodplain zoning policy and legislation, based 
on hazard, risk and vulnerability mapping, flood resilient building codes and embed climate change considerations 
in the design and construction standards of critical infrastructure, will all ensure that strengthen institutional 
capacities and institutional arrangements, tools, technologies, enabling environments and policies, are in place 
within organizations when the project exits, thus ensuring sustainability, scalability and replicability of project 
interventions. 
 
161. Investment in development of long-term climate risk financing: The project is developing fully articulated 
long-term municipal risk management plans for 10-12 highest risk municipalities and 1 canton, and sector 
resilience plans for highest risk sectors, which will set the direction for systematic risk informed sectoral flood risk 
management in the future. In addition, the project will develop long-term risk financing mechanisms and 
investment plans. Cost-benefit assessment methodologies and a socio-economic risk model will be embedded 
into the identification and design of climate proofed structural and non-structural measures and will form the basis 
of municipal investment planning. Risk financing mechanisms to be developed by the project, will ensure that long-
term financial and economic resources are identified and available after GCF assistance ends and will ensure 
cost-effective financing of flood risk reduction measures, and investments over time.  This will result in sustainable 
financing of FRM in B&H.  
 
162. Investment in social and economic assets of vulnerable communities:  The damaging effects of extreme 
events and changes in weather patterns exacerbated by climate change will limit the potential for socioeconomic 
development in vulnerable and isolated communities. However, investing in climate resilient structural and non-
structural measures (Activities 2.2 and 3.3) can help reduce the vulnerability of these areas. Flood defenses that 
are “climate proofed” will have structural stability and functional longevity under climate change. Furthermore, the 
development and implementation of climate-proofing standards for the design and construction of critical 
infrastructure, will strengthen the reliability of infrastructure and, in turn, reduce the impact on communities during 
flood events. Investment in EbA measures provides cost efficiency and long-term sustainability as EBA 
investments  are self-sustaining in the long run, are adaptable, and require reduced OM costs. Hence, EbA 
solutions are an investment in social and economic assets that will provide sustained ecosystem services. This 
project will embed climate risk information and enhance the capacity to prioritize, budget, locate and deploy critical 
infrastructure that is functionally stable under climate hazards. This will help ensure the financial sustainability of 
critical infrastructure climate proofing efforts for the long-term.  
 
163. Investment in natural capital: Securing long-term resilience and safeguard investments and communities 
against climate induced flood risk, by ensuring functional and protected watersheds, stable, climate resilient and 
well-managed natural capital (Activities 2.2 and 2.3), the project will be investing in long-term sustainability of 
communities and their social and economic assets through the investment in EbA solutions, and the derived eco-
system service attendant benefits of improved functions of catchments through effective flood risk management.   
 
164. The project will develop an integrated river-basin development approach and will implement some of the structural 
and non-structural intervention measures in selected high priority areas. These will provide strong replicability 
impact as they will establish the methods, standards and approaches that will work across B&H and define these 
in guidance, legal and policy documents. The potential for scaling up these approaches is therefore significant. 
There is at least a threefold replication potential for the EbA measures, community based EWS, municipal risk 
management plans, natural disasters’ insurance schemes etc.  Having in mind institutional and geographical 
similarities of all Western Balkans countries, there is a regional replication potential for all aspects of the project.

=== PAGE 58 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 54 
 
 
 
 
 
165. Post-project O&M: Co-financing from government and private sector will include budgeted O&M costs for a period 
well beyond the project duration.  In terms of maintenance of the expanded hydrometric network, during and after 
the end of the project (please refer to co-funding letters, Annex 13).   
 
166. In addition, the project will assist relevant government institutions in developing long-term O&M financial planning 
to ensure hydrometric network equipment, FFEWS and all systems implemented under the project will be 
maintained in the long-term. The project interventions have also been designed to strengthen financing and 
implementation of O&M for implemented structural and non-structural measures in the long-term. To this end, the 
project will address current and future requirements for maintenance by developing and embedding CBA, asset 
management and portfolio risk management methods that ensure the systematic identification and prioritization 
of the maintenance costs over the lifespan of the infrastructure.  
 
167. Specifically, the project will provide the following safeguards to financial sustainability: 
• 
Long-term investment planning for FRM including identification of long-term O&M financing needs 
• 
Review budgetary requirements for long-term maintenance of hydrometric network and structural and non-
structural interventions based on climate risk information to be developed by the project, and development of 
a financing model for the long-term maintenance (using cost-benefit modelling to support the financing model) 
• 
Strengthening the approach to O&M for a harmonized O&M framework by implementing capacity development 
in O&M of the optimized hydrometric network 
• 
Improve private sector involvement and partnership in the area of flood risk management.

Capacity Building 
169. Under Activity 1.1 the project will provide training to 50 hydrometric practitioners of HMIs and WAs in 
O&M of up-graded hydrometric stations. 
 
170. Under Activity 1.3 the project will undertake an institutional capacity assessment of FRM institutions and 
develop a capacity development plan for embedding flood hazard and risk modelling approaches and 
FFEWS into relevant institutions in B&H. Institutional capacity gaps and training needs will be assessed, 
and a revised institutional capacity building plan will be developed to address gaps (human, technical and 
financial).  The recruitment and training need to fill capacity gaps will be addressed. The project will 
develop training plans for each technical area of expertise related to climate-induced flood risk assessment 
and management, and FFEWS, and consolidate into an overall capacity development plan.  The long-
term capacity plan for B&H will be developed and will consider options such as the development of 
apprenticeships, internships and voluntary schemes for University students. To address issues of skills 
shortage, skills retention and succession planning, the project will develop approaches which will include 
examining the role of the private sector (consultants, contractors, research institutes) in filling these gaps, 
the use of Continuous Professional Development (CPD) methods involving cross-fertilizing of staff with 
skills across all organizations (e.g. through training in all technical areas before specializing), development 
of standardized and country-wide accepted guidance documents, codes and standards which will enable 
consistency and uniformity of technical approaches.

=== PAGE 63 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 59 
 
 
 
 
 
171. Under Activity 1.3 the project will also undertake capacity building and training for HPP practitioners, 
including specific training on the use of climate information products and services and the use of the new 
HPP models and climate information products in HPP operations. For the agricultural sector, the project 
will support improvement of existing agrometeorological advisory services to farmers with the focus on 
flood risk management by training and embedding training (ToT) with agricultural specialists who will work 
with local farmers to teach them how best to use the weather/flood forecasts within their farming methods, 
and help increase their understanding of how to apply it to their agricultural practices. 
 
172. Under Activity 2.3 the project will undertake training in the methodology and guidance for undertaking risk 
assessment and appraisal of EbA measures; eco-system analysis and evaluation; and eco-system 
valuation methods, and eco-system benefit assessment as part of CBA. 
 
173. 
Under Activity 2.4, the project will develop and fully embed the long-term capacity development 
strategy for all aspects of FRM in B&H.  It will establish training curricula, and rosters of trainers, 
mechanisms and financing strategies for delivering long-term capacity development such as development 
of key partnerships with universities that can be forged to ensure long-term FRM training. The project will 
deliver some of the prioritized training in the areas of:  flood hazard and risk assessment, hydrometry, 
forecasting and modelling, EWSs; vulnerability and risk assessments; river basin flood risk management 
planning and the use of climate information; Under this activity the project will also deliver an awareness 
raising campaign for capacity building at municipal and community levels;  Networking and advocacy; 
Country-wide media campaign on FRM and FFEWS. Under Sub-activity 2.4.3 (See pages 251-252 of FS) 
the project will assist the government of B&H in shifting from ad-hoc project-based awareness and 
education efforts to a planned, consistent and sustainable information and communication system for 
enhanced flood and disaster risk management at the community level.  To this end, it will develop an 
awareness raising campaign which will include the following: Capacity building at municipal and community 
levels:  Emergency response trainings for first responders in cooperation with Civil Protection; Strategic 
FRM 
planning 
and 
FRM

identification 
approaches 
for

DRM guidelines for specific target groups including teachers, municipal authorities, media, women’s 
groups; Capacity building and awareness raising of municipal authorities, local NGOs, CBOs or non-CBO 
community members in Community-based Risk Assessment approaches, Community-based Early 
Warning Systems and gender-responsive Community-based Flood Risk Management; Development of 
‘training of trainers’ programme and a syllabus for topics such as first aid, search and rescue and warning 
dissemination, and example standard specifications for external training providers; Working with the 
appropriate agencies to develop generic educational material and a 5year training programme on flood 
and disaster risk and emergency response for use in school outreach programmes, university courses, 
communities etc. (training material, course notes, plays etc.); Training on how to use the hazard and risk 
maps provided (paper and electronic) to raise awareness but also on how to plan development and other 
activities locally. In addition the project will implement networking and advocacy which will include: 
Organizing annual community forums on FRM and CBEWSs with participation of target communities and 
representatives of vulnerable groups to exchange information, lessons learned, successes and 
impediments; Organizing community-government and public-private dialogues around local risks and risk 
reduction strategies and their financing.  In addition a country-wide media campaign on FRM and FFEWS 
will be developed to include: Develop and deliver awareness raising messages through a number of 
methods including using social media, art and creativity to communicate FRM and FFEWS concepts to 
broader society and to disseminate project lessons and successes; Organizing TV and radio talk shows 
around FRM and FFEWS; Media coverage of project activities; Production of footages, Public Social 
Announcements (PSAs) of short documentaries around FRM and project achievement; Training for media 
on FRM and FFEWS and annual essay/media article awards competitions.  The project will utilize its 
partnerships with Society of Red Cross (RC) B&H and IFRC to develop, deliver and embed the community-
based training and awareness raising.    
 
174. 
Under Activity 3.1 the project will provide technical advice and training of insurance sector 
practitioners in the development of insurance products packages from natural disasters adjusted to the 
needs of the assessed sectors and insurers.

other DRR institutions, as well as national and regional research institutions, universities and training
