 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
236. The project was screened against UNDP’s Social and Environmental Standards using the UNDP Social and 
Environmental Screening Procedure (SESP), to identify any potential social and environmental risks that could 
arise from project activities. An Environmental and Social Assessment Report (ESAR) was prepared to assess the 
potential risks in more detail (see Annex 6).The ESAR has been prepared using publicly available information, 
project reference documents, consultation with stakeholders such as government, NGOs and project development 
team members, and field-visits by a safeguard specialist to representative sites.   
237. The risk profile of the project has been determined to be moderate (Category B).  The risks are considered 
acceptable and manageable through the application of mitigation measures.  The ESAR includes and 
Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), which provides an outline of the types of mitigation 
measures that are likely to be required when implementing the project.   
238. Through delivery of the project’s inter-related outputs, communities in B&H will be better equipped to adapt to 
climate impacts, particularly flooding.  The project will deliver sustainable development co-benefits including 
enhanced ecosystem services, gender responsive livelihood enhancement and job creation, and improved 
resilience of communities’ physical assets. 
 
Environment 
239. Physical impacts will be primarily associated with with construction activities for the river works, and access to land 
for both river works and forestry activities, particularly the implementation of the priority risk reduction interventions 
at the sites proposed under Activity 3.3. Construction impacts will be temporary in nature, such as localised 
erosion/sedimentation, noise and dust from construction equipment, and possible changes to people’s ability to 
move within the locale during construction.  Permanant infrastructure will be designed to minimise long-term 
impacts. The implementation of the ESMP will ensure that these impacts are satisfactorily managed 
240. The majority of the construction works involve building river infrastructure.  It is anticipated that most materials, 
particularly rock, will come from existing quarries near the sites.  Other material requirements will be minimal.  The 
proposed river works will not impact on critical ecosystems and/or habitats.  The rivers have been significantly 
impacted by both anthropogenic activities and climate induced events in the past.  Some riparian vegetation will 
be lost at some of the sites.  
241. The project is addressing climate-induced flood risks by introducing FRM and CCA measures.  In general, the 
project is providing key environmental protection benefits through the introduction of disaster risk reduction, climate 
change adaptation and protection of people, property and the environment from major hazards. In the long-run the 
project will bring about significant environmental benefits by increasing the country’s resilience to climate-induced 
flood disasters and thus, enabling its population to better protect national assets, including environmental assets 
(land, forest and land resources). 
242. Environmental co-benefits mainly relate to EbA strategies such as riparian plantings and agro-forestry which will 
provide water retention and functions; regulation of hydrological flows (buffer runoff, soil infiltration, groundwater 
recharge, maintenance of base flows); natural hazard mitigation (e.g. flood prevention, peak flow reduction, soil 
erosion and landslide control); increased streambed stabilization resulting in decreased erosion, habitat 
preservation, and reforestation. Environmental impacts associated with the project are considered acceptable due 
to the nature of the interventions and the ability to manage impacts through implementation of mitigation measures, 
while social benefits are significant and can provide long-term improvements to the lives of communities in the 
target areas (see Section D). 
 
Social 
243. The project is expected to directly benefit approximately 26% of the population.  Project interventions will be 
undertaken in areas of B&H where communities are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The 
project is targeting those most vulnerable to flood risk and the impact potential of the enhanced flood risk 
management and intervention measures is high and will safeguard vulnerable communities and their economic 
assets from these climate induced disasters.  Government land will be used for structural interventions, so the 
project does not require any land acquisition and/or resettlement.       
244. The project will also improve the capacity of government and communities to plan, design and manage rural 
infrastructure that is climate resilient. By enhancement of the existing legislative and regulatory FRM framework in 
order to mainstream the climate-induced flood risk management into sectoral planning for agriculture, hydropower, 
critical infrastructure and spatial planning, the project will directly protect and save lives, increase food production 
safety, and flood risk informed local development and land use.

=== PAGE 113 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 109 
 
 
 
 
245. The approach applied within the project will underpin the floodplain planning and development control to achieve 
basin-wide climate resilience to increasing flood risks and will result in a paradigm shift from uncontrolled floodplain 
development to climate-risk informed and controlled floodplain usage. 
246. It is anticipated that based on the communities’ needs, the projects will be fully accepted.  None the less, the project 
has developed a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) to deal with any complaints and issues that may arise 
because of the project.  The GRM has been designed to ensure that an individual and/or group are not financially 
impacted by the process of making a complaint and/or grievance.  The GRM complies with Bosnian and UNDP 
Safeguard procedures.  Furthermore, the GRM allows individuals and/or groups to also file a complaint with the 
Office of Anticorruption and Integrity within the UNDP or utilise the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism should 
they have any concerns as to corruption, unethical behaviour or where they believe their complaint or grievance 
has not been adequately addressed. 
 
Gender and social inclusion 
Gender 
247. The project will directly benefit the most vulnerable parts of the population, particularly women who are 
disproportionately affected by flooding and disasters in general. In B&H there are significant gender inequalities, 
fuelled by traditional gender roles.  The project has been designed with attention paid to gender and social 
inclusion. UNDP64 has identified that the most vulnerable women in the country are: the women living below the 
poverty line, single mothers, Roma women, internally displaced women, minority returnees and LGBTI women. 
The gender analysis undertaken at the project design phase, acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming 
throughout implementation, and builds on stakeholder consultation, existing analytical documentation and data 
from projects currently being implemented, and national statistics where available.  
248. The project will ensure that women are primary stakeholders and will therefore need to be involved in decisions on 
the types of solutions that are implemented.  The project will safeguard local communities and their assets from 
climate disasters with particular attention to women, and other vulnerable groups.  The Gender Assessment and 
Action Plan prepared for the project outlines positive actions that will be taken by the project. 
249. The project will ensure that women are empowered to benefit from the structural interventions that will mitigate 
flood events and will be included in consultations during design to ensure that the structures will not impact 
negatively on gender equality.  With regard to EBA approaches, the project will implement agro-forestry, which will 
enable and empower women to diversify agricultural activities, thus building women’s resilience to cope with 
climate change impacts.   
250. Along with a multi-year climate resilient municipal investment plan, the project will formulate and implement gender 
sensitive community preparedness plans in selected municipalities (10-12) in Vrbas, Una-Sana and Bosna basins. 
The preparedness plans will be based on needs assessment of both genders and will comprise of gender sensitive 
actions including access to knowledge (trainings), information and resources that will increase capability of both 
genders for timely and effective actions to avoid the loss of lives and to reduce the damages of flooding during the 
event. 
251. 
UNDP will request that contractors, suppliers, and partners adhere to zero tolerance for sexual exploitation and 
harassment (SEAH) and commit to taking adequate action if faced with SEAH allegations, in the absence of which, 
contractual arrangements can be terminated. UNDP has a standalone Code of Ethics which applies to all staff and 
contractors and defines the ethical standards applicable to all, citing the specific policy references. UNDP is 
committed to creating and maintaining a working environment that respects the inherent dignity of all persons, 
affording them the opportunity to reach their fullest potential and empowering them to deliver the best possible 
results for UNDP and the people we serve. To that effect, UNDP has further implemented policies for protection 
against Harassment, Sexual Harassment, Discrimination and abuse of Authority, as well as special measures for 
protection from sexual exploitation and sexual abuse.  These policies are further backed by a Policy for Protection 
against Retaliation against individuals holding UNDP assignments/contracts (i.e., staff members, interns, UN 
Volunteers and contractors) who have, in good faith, properly reported allegations of misconduct, or who have 
cooperated with a duly authorized audit or investigation. 
Other vulnerable groups 
 
64 https://www.undp.org/bosnia-herzegovina/publications/intersectional-analysisdifferential-impact-covid-19-crisis-and-inflation-
womenbosnia-and-herzegovina

=== PAGE 114 ===

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 110 
 
 
 
 
252. 
In Bosnia and Herzegovina, several groups are particularly vulnerable to flood risk. These groups may face 
higher levels of exposure and have limited capacities to cope with and recover from the impacts of flooding. The 
main vulnerable groups are: 
• 
Socioeconomically Disadvantaged Communities: Socioeconomically disadvantaged communities, including 
low-income households, marginalized groups, and those living in informal settlements or substandard housing, 
are often more vulnerable to flood risk. Limited resources, lack of infrastructure, and inadequate access to 
services make it challenging for these communities to effectively prepare for, respond to, and recover from 
floods 
• 
Rural and Agricultural Communities: Rural communities, particularly those engaged in agriculture and 
subsistence farming, are highly susceptible to flood risk. Floods can lead to crop losses, damage to livestock, 
destruction of infrastructure (e.g., irrigation systems), and disruption of livelihoods. The dependence on 
agricultural activities and limited access to alternative income sources amplify their vulnerability. 
• 
Elderly Population: The elderly population faces heightened vulnerability to flood risk due to physical limitations, 
limited mobility, and underlying health conditions. These factors can hinder their ability to evacuate, seek 
shelter, or access necessary assistance during and after flooding events. Additionally, the elderly population 
may face challenges in recovering and rebuilding their lives after a flood 
• 
Children and Youth: Children and youth are a vulnerable group during floods due to their dependency on 
caregivers, increased health risks, and potential disruptions to education and social support networks. 
Displacement, loss of shelter, separation from family members, and exposure to hazardous conditions can 
have long-lasting impacts on their well-being, development, and future opportunities 
• 
Ethnic and Minority Groups: Ethnic and minority groups, such as Roma communities, particularly those living 
in remote or marginalized areas, may face disproportionate vulnerability to flood risk. Limited access to 
resources, infrastructure, and decision-making processes can hinder their capacity to prepare for and respond 
to floods. Cultural and language barriers may also impede effective communication and coordination during 
emergencies. 
• 
People with Disabilities: Individuals with disabilities may face additional challenges during floods, such as 
limited mobility, sensory impairments, or reliance on assistive devices. Barriers in physical infrastructure, lack 
of inclusive emergency plans, and limited access to information and support services can exacerbate their 
vulnerability and reduce their ability to safely evacuate or access necessary assistance. 
Understanding the specific vulnerabilities of gender and other socially and economically excluded groups is crucial for 
developing inclusive and targeted flood risk management strategies. It is also important to address the intersecting 
vulnerabilities and promote social equity in building resilience to flood risk in B&H. During project development, a 
number of these vulnerabilities were included in the socio-economic assessment of flood risk modelling and 
assessment based on socio-economic and census data. During implementation and as part of the ESIA and ESMP, 
measures will be tailored to ensure their participation, access to early warning systems, evacuation plans, and post-
flood recovery support.  
 
Conclusion 
253. Environmental impacts associated with the project are considered acceptable due to the nature of the interventions 
and the ability to manage impacts through implementation of mitigation measures, while social benefits are 
significant and can provide long-term improvements to the lives of communities in the target areas.   
 
254. Budgeting for environmental interventions and the application of mitigation measures to enhance positive impacts 
for the project is an investment in the future as it will reduce the environmental and social liability at local, state and 
national levels.  The end result of this expenditure will be that there will be reduced river sedimentation and erosion; 
stronger and improved resilience to the impacts of climate change, healthier ecosystems, more knowledgeable 
communities and overall improvement in the quality of life of the population as an investment in the future of the 
people of Bosnia and Herzegovina, which if implemented as per the project proposal, will be repaid may times over 
through reduced long-term operation and maintenance costs of implementing the project. 
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
255. The project will address specific gender-based vulnerabilities as a key requirement of enhanced of climate change 
adaptation strategies and actions.  As a result of the project implementation more lives, property and productive 
assets will be secured from the impacts of climate induced disasters.  While all B&H population will benefit from

=== PAGE 115 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 111 
 
 
 
 
the project, the gender gap will decrease as women’s benefits will be relatively bigger given greater dependence 
of their livelihoods on natural resources. During the proposal development individual gender-specific impacts and 
needs have been examined through the lenses of vulnerability, capacities and voices and this will continue 
throughout project implementation as detailed in the Gender Action Plan (Annex 8). This approach will ensure 
support to most vulnerable social groups among which women in B&H are specifically recognized. By enabling 
equal participation of men and women, the project will ensure that climate change adaptation is more effective and 
sustainable. 
 
256. Assessment of the human rights within the B&H legal framework in latest decades reveals that significant progress 
has been made in the application of the principle of the gender equality, which is a result of activities taken by 
gender institutional mechanisms but also due to civil society activism. However, the Gender Assessment and 
Gender Action Plan (Annex 8) outline several social phenomena that are increasing gender-based vulnerability 
especially in conditions of climate induced disastrous floods in B&H. 
257. The vulnerability of women in B&H is caused by social and economic reasons which are deeply rooted in traditional 
norms of the society. Representation of women in legislative and executive authorities as well as in managerial 
positions, at all levels, remains unsatisfactory.  Hence there is reduced participation of women in decision making 
including economic, environmental and social issues. Due to gender stereotypes women with secondary and 
university education are also more likely to be unemployed than men with the same education. Out of total number 
of employees only 37% are women. One working hour of women in B&H is 10% less paid than men.  Also, great 
prevalence of traditional socio-cultural gender norms supports male ownership.  Men account for over 70 percent 
of landownership and own 74 percent of dwellings. Women have lower financial inclusion by almost 10% compared 
to man. Social gender stereotypes that restrict women’s involvement outside of household and family are even 
more deeply rooted in rural areas. 
 
258. The existing gender inequality factors (e.g. limited engagement of women in planning and decision making) and 
traditional distribution of gender roles in families and communities call for tailoring and targeting of the project 
solutions to outreach beneficiaries of both genders equally. In order to address these vulnerabilities and more 
effectively involve women in FRM processes the project has dedicated special attention to gender issues under 
activities: 1.2, 1.3, 1.4, 2.4, 3.1, 3.2. The project will assess gender-specific vulnerabilities  to flood risks through 
gender responsive socio-economic data collection, ensure equal participation and decision making as well as 
information access and warning dissemination services. Further, the project will ensure women are involved in the 
processes of capacity building for climate induced hazard risk assessment within the FRM at all levels, but also in 
the development of public/private collaboration on building resilience by climate risk finance mechanisms. 
 
259. At Project inception, additional gender analysis will be undertaken to ensure that the baseline data set is sex- and 
age-disaggregated and adequate for assessment of the gender impacts of the Project. Progress reports will provide 
periodic updates on the effect of the Project on women, and regular UNDP review missions and the midterm review 
mission will review and monitor the gender impacts. The Gender Action Plan includes a number of 
recommendations aimed at ensuring that the project: 
• 
narrows gender inequality.  
• 
addresses the needs and constraints of women, girls, men, and boys.  
• 
avoid any risks of adverse gender impacts;  
• 
ensure women’s participation, promotes their leadership qualities; and  
• 
ensure women are included as planners, co–implementers and agents of change.  
 
As part of the implementation of the Gender Action Plan the project will:  
(i) Carry out gender responsive vulnerability assessment informed by gender analysis:  When carrying out group 
discussions and in-depth interviews with key stakeholders, the project will ensure an equal representation of 
women, elderly people, disabled or family members of disabled, IDPs, community leaders and government 
officials.  
(ii) Ensure collection of sex- and age-disaggregated data for project indicators: Gender responsive and sex-
disaggregated reporting will be further introduced beyond the project level at the national level through the 
multi-hazard disaster risk information and knowledge system to be designed by the project.   
(iii) Enhance gender considerations in disaster preparedness activities based on gender analysis: Men and 
women of different age groups, as well as those from vulnerable communities will be involved in planning and 
implementation. Community consultancy groups with at least 30 percent representation of less represented 
sex will be established. The project will ensure that information on hazards, their character, probability of 
occurrence, threats to life, possible impact on livelihood, houses, crops and livestock and on protecting

=== PAGE 116 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 112 
 
 
 
 
measures is reaching both women and men through appropriately tailored channels. Gender Mechanisms 
representative will be engaged in disaster preparedness activities. 
(iv) Warrant universal access to disaster warnings: The project will tailor warning to the gender-differentiated 
needs and capabilities of specific population groups, such as children, senior citizens, IDPs, the sick and 
persons with disabilities.   
(v) Mainstream gender considerations and engagement of women in resilience building activities targeting 30 
percent representation of less represented sex in capacity building and training activities supported by the 
project. The project will ensure that women have equal access to information and livelihood generating 
activities facilitated by the project. The project will strengthen social capital through creating community 
groups for planning for, safeguarding from and mitigating adverse effects of natural disasters with at least 30 
percent less represented sex participation. 
(vi) Conduct gender mainstreaming trainings and build capacity of national stakeholders targeted by project and 
responsible for various disaster risk reduction functions  
(vii) Engage women in decision-making: the project will ensure at least 30 percent representation and active 
participation of less represented sex in project stakeholder consultations, local and national decision-making 
bodies established and/or facilitated by the project. The project will secure participation of the Gender 
Institutional Mechanism representatives in the consultations and decision-making.  
(viii) 
Project staff: The project will ensure that men and women are adequately represented in the project 
implementation team, staff members will be trained on gender mainstreaming and on social vulnerability 
approach. The Project team leaders will ensure that all specialists are briefed on their responsibilities in 
relation to the Gender Action Plan upon mobilization. 
 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
260. The financial management and procurement of this project will follow UNDP financial rules and regulations 
available 
here: 
https://info.undp.org/global/documents/frm/Financial-Rules-and-Regulations_E.pdf. 
Further 
guidance is outlined in the financial resources management section of the UNDP Programme and Operations 
Policies and Procedures available at https://info.undp.org/global/popp/frm/Pages/introduction.aspx. 
 
261. UNDP has comprehensive procurement policies in place as outlined in the ‘Contracts and Procurement’ section of 
UNDP’s Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures (POPP).  The policies outline formal procurement 
standards and guidelines across each phase of the procurement process, and they apply to all procurements in 
UNDP.  See here:  https://info.undp.org/global/popp/cap/Pages/Introduction.aspx. Procurement of services and 
goods will be done in a cost effective and reliable way and by applying following principles: Best Value for Money, 
which consists of the selection of the offer that best meets the end-users’ needs and that presents the best return 
on investment; Fairness, Integrity and Transparency, which ensures that competitive processes are fair, open, 
and rules-based. All potential vendors will be treated equally, and the process will feature clear evaluation criteria, 
unambiguous solicitation instructions, realistic requirements, and rules and procedures that are easy to 
understand; Effective International Competition, understood as giving all potential vendors timely and adequate 
information on UNDP requirements, as well as equal opportunity to participate in procurement actions; and In the 
best interest of UNDP, which means that any business transactions must conform to the mandates and principles 
of UNDP and the United Nations. As outlined in the Procurement Plan, procurement of goods and services will be 
carried out by UNDP and the UNDP procurement procedures will be applied (POPP). Additional details on the 
UNDP procurement threthholds and methods are provided in the Annex 10. Procurement Plan 
 
262. The project will be implemented following the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) following DIM guidelines. 
 
263. The project will be audited following the UNDP financial rules and regulations noted above and applicable audit 
guidelines and policies. According to the current audit policies, UNDP will be appointing the auditors. In UNDP 
scheduled audits are performed annually, during the programme cycle as per UNDP assurance/audit plans, on the 
basis of the implementing partner's risk rating and UNDP’s guidelines. The project financial statements will comply 
with international accounting standards. A scheduled audit is used to determine whether the funds transferred to 
the implementing partner were used for the appropriate purpose and in accordance with the work plan. A scheduled 
audit can consist of a financial audit or an internal control audit.  
 
264. Financial Sustainability. The government’s co-finance covers O&M costs during the 6 years of the project’s 
implementation duration, and the government and HPP partner are committed to cover O&M costs of equipment

=== PAGE 117 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 113 
 
 
 
 
and flood defences installed during the project over the 20 years of investment lifetime, thus ensuring continued 
operation and financial sustainability of the project’s proposed investments. Details of the financial analysis are 
presented in Annex XII (b). In addition, the project is developing risk-based investment planning frameworks at the 
municipal and contonal levels, climate risk financing and risk transfer mechanisms.  These interventions will help 
to identify and plan the financing that is needed to address climate risks to communities, develop and implement 
long-term public and private sector climate finacing, as well as instruments for transferring risk and building 
financial resilience of households and key sector players. All of these interventions will contribute to the financial 
sustainability of the project.   
G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal  
Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information. 
 
☒ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may 
be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence. 
☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may 
not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited 
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes: 
•

an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and 
•

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential 
information.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP216 
Proposal name: 
Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina 
Accredited entity: 
United Nations Development Programme

Bosnia and Herzegovina
