I. 
Overall assessment of the Secretariat 
1. 
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks: 
Strengths 
Points of caution 
The direct benefits will be extended to 799,445 
people, accounting for 25 per cent of the total 
population, with a focus on the most vulnerable 
population. 
Political instability could pose a risk that 
requires careful management.  
Scaling up successful innovative flood risk 
management (FRM) technologies and 
implementing a nationwide, impact-based Flood 
Forecasting and Early Warning System (FFEWS), 
which will lead to improved warning times and 
reduced losses. 
The project’s strong inclination towards 
scaling up and replicating proposed 
innovations and knowledge products beyond 
the project areas will require great attention 
to the technical soundness and flexibility of, 
in particular, innovative FRM technologies 
and FFEWS to incorporate the needs and 
vulnerability aspects of communities and 
institutions outside the scope of the project.  
 
Engaging the private sector in climate information 
services and FRM activities and facilitating the 
increased use of climate risk information in its 
operations.  
 
GCF investment of USD 14.4 million will leverage 
an additional USD 58.29 million in co-financing 
from both public and private sectors. The 
government of Bosnia and Herzegovina will 
contribute USD 30.74 million in co-financing.  
 
2. 
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the 
terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XXI, titled “List of 
proposed conditions and recommendations”. 
II. 
Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

=== PAGE 126 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 126 
 
 
2.1 
Project background  
3. 
Bosnia and Herzegovina, identified as a middle-income country, has an estimated 
population of 3.531 million and spans an area of 51,209.2 km². During the 1992–1995 conflict, 
the country underwent severe destruction. In subsequent years, it has been navigating a 
complicated landscape of post-war rebuilding, along with economic and social revitalization. 
The conflict significantly compromised the country’s flood risk management capabilities, 
inflicting direct harm on flood control infrastructure and hydrological monitoring stations, 
while also creating hazardous minefields around these facilities. Adding to these difficulties is 
the country’s gradual economic transition towards a market-based system, demanding 
enhanced governance and structural changes.   
4. 
Around a quarter of the population of Bosnia and Herzegovina faces a high risk of 
flooding exacerbated by climate change, which has led to increased rainfall intensity and 
unpredictability, resulting in more frequent and severe flooding events that impact lives, 
property and essential infrastructure. Key economic sectors such as agriculture, water 
management and hydropower are especially vulnerable. Between 2006 and 2012, flooding 
accounted for 62 per cent of all natural disaster-related damages across the countries. This was 
followed by a significant flood event in 2010 that led to damages worth USD 200 million, or 
approximately 1 per cent of gross domestic product. An even more catastrophic flooding event 
occurred in May 2014, the worst in 150 years, affecting a quarter of the nation’s land and 
causing a staggering USD 2.7 billion in damages, equivalent to 15 per cent of Bosnia and 
Herzegovina’s gross domestic product. These floods have disproportionately affected 
vulnerable segments of the population, including the rural poor, war returnees and displaced 
persons, who often live in flood-prone areas.  
5. 
The objective of the project is to seek to reduce the growing vulnerabilities of 
communities and livelihoods in Bosnia and Herzegovina to heightened risks of climate-triggered 
floods. The project is designed to establish an integrated and innovative, gender-responsive, 
climate-resilient approach to FRM. It aims to fortify the institutional, technical and financial 
frameworks for the sustained implementation of comprehensive FRM strategies, which include 
a blend of structural interventions, non-structural measures as well as ecosystem-based 
approaches. Additionally, the project will focus on improving the utilization of climate 
information, flood forecasting, early warning and emergency response systems to heighten the 
resilience and adaptive capacities of communities at risk. This initiative is aligned with the 
United Nations multi-partner initiative Early Warnings for All (EW4All) programme, launched 
in March 2022, and aims to contribute to the execution of the EWS4All Executive Action Plan, 
developed in March 2023. The project outlines two outcomes and three outputs:  
(a) 
Outcome 1: Climate-informed gender-responsive impact-based FFEWS and increased 
generation and use of climate data reduce vulnerability to flood-related disasters; 
(i) 
Output 1: Fully integrated impact-based FFEWS facilitates timely preparation 
and response;  
(b) 
Outcome 2: Vulnerable communities in Bosnia and Herzegovina including women, 
children, persons with disabilities and older persons are better protected from and less 
exposed to climate-induced flood risks through a combination of structural and non-
structural flood risk reduction solutions; 
(i) 
Output 2: Non-structural flood risk reduction measures and nature-based 
solutions mainstreamed in sectoral policies and plans and effectively contribute 
to the protection of people and livelihoods from climate-induced flood risk; and 
(ii) 
Output 3:  Climate-proof flood protection measures are scaled-up through new 
and improved national and local investment frameworks increasing the 
resilience of the most vulnerable groups to climate-induced flooding.

=== PAGE 127 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 127 
 
 
6. 
Aimed at impacting a significant segment of the population, the proposed project targets 
799,445 direct beneficiaries by the end of the project, representing 25 per cent of the total 
population. It will prevent loss of life, property, critical infrastructure and economic assets, and 
will build resilience to flooding in the most vulnerable communities.  
7. 
The total project financing amounts to USD 72.69 million, with a request for GCF grants 
of USD 14.4 million. The remaining sum of USD 58.23 million will come from a diverse range of 
co-financing partners, which include the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina and UNDP.  
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis 
8. 
The project objective will be achieved through three complementary outputs that 
comprise its three components.  
Component 1: Climate-informed impact-based FFEWS and an increased generation and use of 
climate data to reduce vulnerability to flood-related disasters (total cost: USD 14.65 million; GCF 
cost: USD 5.095 million) 
9. 
The project aims to enhance the hydrometeorological monitoring network by covering a 
broader array of hydrometric variables impacted by climate change. Special focus will be on 
placed areas with the highest risk, such as Category I rivers and torrents. Technical support will 
be offered to integrate hydro power plants' hydrometric networks into the national grid. A 
sustainable operation and maintenance (O&M) system will also be set up, financed by both the 
public and private sectors.  
10. 
This component will refine existing hazard and risk models to include a more 
comprehensive approach. Activities will include extending models to cover whole basins, 
including category II tributaries, and improving hydrological modelling. The goal is to create an 
effective reservoir operation strategy that balances flood risk, dam safety and power generation. 
The project will incorporate hydro power plant operations into flood hazard and risk modelling 
in key basins and will produce specialized climate risk information for the hydropower sector, 
especially focusing on the Neretva and Trebisnjica River Sub-basins.  
11. 
Building on existing FFEWS, this project will advance information and communications 
technology systems to support real-time forecasting and warning. The new FFEWS will cover 
hydro-energy intensive river basins such as Neretva and Trebisnjica, and update existing 
models for the Una-Sana River Basin. Further enhancements will include advanced 
meteorological forecasting capabilities based on numerical weather prediction models. 
12. 
To ensure the sustainability of these initiatives, the project will deliver extensive 
training and capacity-building programmes. It will streamline communication between different 
agencies to avoid duplication and inefficiencies and establish standard operating procedures 
(SOPs), communication protocols and codes of conduct, including “Last-mile” communication 
protocols, protocols and SOPs defining the forecast thresholds for forecast-based financing. The 
project will also develop operational maintenance of the hydrometric network. It will assess and 
further develop specific data-sharing/communication protocols for impact-based FFEWS and 
climate-resilient FRM in coordination with the national adaptation plan project and will 
implement these protocols across relevant institutions. 
Component 2: Non-structural flood risk reduction measures and nature-based solutions 
mainstreamed within sectoral policies and plans and effectively contribute to protection of people 
and livelihoods from climate-induced flood risk (total cost: USD 27.67 million; GCF cost: USD 6.74 
million) 
13. 
The second component of the project is focused on elevating key FRM policies. This will 
be accomplished by enhancing existing legal and regulatory frameworks to incorporate climate-
responsive FRM across various sectors, such as agriculture, forestry, hydropower and spatial

=== PAGE 128 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 128 
 
 
planning. At the state level, a holistic, cross-entity strategy will be adopted, particularly for 
shared basins. This integrated approach necessitates the participation of key stakeholders, 
improved coordination, and the ratification of relevant regional and multilateral environmental 
agreements. The project will also revise existing regulations to accommodate the challenges 
posed by climate-induced floods. Through these activities, local governments will be enabled to 
formulate evidence-based, climate-resilient policies and strategies, grounded in comprehensive 
impact assessments and cost–benefit analyses. 
14. 
This project component will incorporate community-based ecosystem-based adaptation 
(EbA) solutions aimed at mitigating the impacts of severe flooding, particularly in the Vrbas 
River Basin. The project will expand upon the successful EbA methods previously piloted by the 
UNDP/Special Climate Change Fund Vrbas River initiative. The Vrbas River Basin, unique in 
Bosnia and Herzegovina for its existing torrential flood hazard maps and evaluated cost-
effective EbA strategies, will be prioritized for the implementation of high-impact ecological 
measures. Additionally, one key issue contributing to sediment build-up is the existing network 
of forest roads, initially carved out during wartime for illegal logging. Though current legislation 
largely prevents the expansion of such roads for timber extraction, their initial construction has 
led to significant material run-off, affecting river networks usually the mainstream of the River 
Vrbas. 
15. 
The project aims to establish a comprehensive framework in Bosnia and Herzegovina 
for applying climate-resilient EbA approaches to flood-risk management. It will adapt well-
tested flood management strategies from other countries and compile best practices. These 
insights will be consolidated into countrywide guidelines, technical specifications, standards 
and protocols, and will be incorporated into national policies. The project will also strengthen 
institutional capacities in identifying and implementing these EbA approaches to flood risk 
management measures.  
16. 
The project will support the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina in transitioning 
from ad-hoc project-based awareness and education to a more systematic and sustainable 
approach to flood and disaster risk education. A long-term awareness-raising campaign will be 
developed and executed. This will include nationwide training programmes focused on flood 
risk management, targeting practitioners, decision makers, local communities and emergency 
responders. The project will also formulate a sustainable capacity development plan, enhancing 
training in FRM and climate risk information within existing educational institutions. This 
strategy will include a Train-the-Trainers programme, foster key partnerships with academic 
institutions, and establish mechanisms for long-term capacity-building, building on the 
groundwork laid by the initial Vrbas project.  
Component 3: Climate-proof flood protection measures scaled-up through new and improved 
national and local investment frameworks increasing resilience of the most vulnerable groups to 
climate-induced flooding (total cost: USD 26.52 million; GCF cost: USD 1.88 million) 
17. 
Component 3 aims to bolster the resilience of community livelihoods by amplifying 
flood protection measures that are resilient to climate change. This will be realized by 
formulating an Investment Framework designed specifically to reduce and manage climate-
induced flood risks, which will involve both public sector and private sector participation in 
climate risk financing and risk transfer mechanisms. The planned activities will establish these 
financing and risk transfer mechanisms after conducting thorough assessments of 
socioeconomic risks, damages and losses. These initiatives will be largely co-financed by the 
national insurance sector. 
18. 
Building on the climate risk-sensitive budgeting mechanisms developed at the municipal 
level under the national adaptation plan project, this component will explore avenues for 
integrating such budgeting practices into Bosnia and Herzegovina's existing public financial 
management framework. This will leverage current strengths while addressing any bottlenecks

=== PAGE 129 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 129 
 
 
in the budgeting process that obstruct climate risk-sensitive budgeting for flood risk 
management. Key tools for this effort will include damage and loss modelling to quantify the 
existing cost of disasters, thereby informing resource allocation within the FRM framework. The 
component will also introduce value-for-money analysis, utilizing cost–benefit data to 
underscore the advantages (and higher value-for-money) of greater spending on FRM, 
preparedness and mitigation. 
19. 
This component will orchestrate a comprehensive engagement programme involving 
state institutions, insurance and reinsurance firms, and financial and capital market entities. 
The goal is to forge pivotal public–private partnerships aimed at augmenting and scaling up 
investments in climate-resilient initiatives. Legislative and governance interventions will be 
executed to reinforce financial institutions, enabling them to offer climate-responsive financing 
options and products within existing markets, thereby ensuring the long-term viability of the 
investments with the potential for future scaling. Additionally, financial institutions, including 
microfinance entities, will receive specialized training and technical guidance for the 
development of lending products. This will allow them to integrate climate risk data into their 
lending processes, thereby optimizing performance and reducing risks. Furthermore, loans with 
favourable terms will be provided to climate-responsive enterprises in Bosnia and Herzegovina, 
improving upon current market conditions in aspects such as interest rates, loan periods and 
credit criteria.  
Project management (total cost: USD 3.85 million; GCF cost: USD 0.68 million) 
20. 
The portion of the project management cost covered by GCF constitutes less than 5 per 
cent of the total GCF funding requested and is in compliance with GCF’s policy on fees.   
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria  
3.1 
Impact potential                                                                               Scale: High 
21. 
The initiative aims to mitigate flood risks for about 799,445 people, or 25 per cent of the 
nation's total population, who are currently vulnerable to climate change. By 2050, it is 
estimated that this will extend to 902,906 people or roughly 26 per cent of the entire 
population. This project is particularly aimed at safeguarding the most flood-vulnerable 
populations in 69 specified municipalities, making up 44 per cent of their inhabitants. The 
implementation of enhanced flood risk management is expected to have a high impact, 
preserving not only lives but also economic assets and infrastructure. Furthermore, EbA 
strategies will bolster both community resilience and ecological safeguards within targeted 
river basins. 
22. 
The project is geared towards fortifying resilience and improving the livelihoods of at-
risk demographics, specifically women, children, and senior citizens. Women make up 51.7 per 
cent of the affected population, whereas those under 15 and over 65 account for 15.2 per cent 
and 13.7 per cent, respectively. Economic analyses suggest that the initiative will yield an 
estimated annual benefit of USD 21 million upon full implementation, with the most significant 
gains seen in the protection of household assets. Geographically, the Bosna and Neretva River 
Sub-basins stand to benefit the most. The resilience of physical infrastructure across three key 
river basins in Bosnia and Herzegovina will also be heightened.  
23. 
In addition to local benefits, the project also aims for broader climate resilience through 
the adoption of FRM technologies and the integration of climate information into institutional 
frameworks in Bosnia and Herzegovina. The introduction of floodplain zoning policies, attuned 
to the specific vulnerabilities of local communities, aims to reduce their flood risk exposure 
significantly.

=== PAGE 130 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 130 
 
 
24. 
Stakeholder consultations have informed the project's design, with particular attention 
paid to the unique vulnerabilities of women, ethnic minorities, the persons with disabilities and 
older persons. The collection of gender-sensitive socioeconomic data will be an ongoing 
process. A Gender Action Plan (GAP) will further anchor the project in fulfilling Bosnia and 
Herzegovina's commitments to gender equality and international protocols on gender-
responsive climate adaptation and disaster risk measures. The GAP will leverage insights from 
prior initiatives, like the UNDP Vrbas project, and will include metrics and tools for monitoring 
progress in gender equality.  
3.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
                                  Scale: Medium to High 
25. 
The project  aims to revolutionize existing flood risk management procedures in Bosnia 
and Herzegovina by enhancing institutional effectiveness at various levels, disseminating 
reliable climate information and early warnings, deepening the understanding of disaster risk, 
and integrating non-structural, ecosystem-based risk management methods. The project also 
plans to collaborate with the private sector and bolster the resilience of both public and private 
investments to climate change, aiming to make flood risk management more integral to 
development planning. Furthermore, the project will cultivate new skill sets by employing 
integrated techniques and technologies to evaluate flood-related risks and formulate innovative 
risk management and mitigation strategies.  
26. 
The project aspires to make robust climate information and early warning services 
widely accessible. It intends to broaden climate risk information for FRM to extend the reach of 
existing hazard and risk modelling to include flash floods and hydroelectric power plants, 
thereby enriching the spectrum of hazard, risk and vulnerability information. The innovative 
introduction of forecast-based financing mechanisms will facilitate early, proactive measures, 
thereby enhancing the efficiency of response efforts and potentially reducing loss and damage.  
27. 
This will mark the first instance of a comprehensive, large-scale FFEWS being 
implemented in the nation. Innovative measures of observational tools, forecasting systems and 
digital technologies like Internet of Things and data analytics will be used to empower local 
communities to undertake their own disaster risk reduction measures. This complements the 
national EWS and offers potential for duplication in other locales. The project also seeks to 
partner with the private sector to create specialized climate services for sectors like 
hydropower and agriculture, thereby enhancing business continuity planning and private sector 
engagement.  
28. 
The plan underscores the importance of knowledge dissemination and collective 
learning. A nationwide flood risk management platform is envisioned to facilitate 
interdisciplinary coordination and offer policy guidance on flood risk management, drawing 
participation from public, private and civil sectors. Activity 2.4 is designed to offer extensive 
training programmes on flood risk management for various stakeholders, including decision 
makers and emergency responders. It also aims to bolster research and development in flood 
risk management via academic collaborations.  
29. 
The proposal is fully aligned with the United Nations Secretary-General’s EW4All 
initiatives, encapsulating all its four pillars: disaster risk knowledge and management; 
detection, observation, monitoring, analysis and forecasting; warning dissemination and 
communication; and preparedness and response capabilities.   
3.3 
Sustainable development potential                                            Scale: High 
30. 
The project is poised to deliver multiple co-benefits including enhanced ecosystem 
services, gender-responsive livelihood enhancement and job creation, and improved resilience

=== PAGE 131 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 131 
 
 
of communities’ physical assets. One of the notable economic advantages stems from averting 
the socioeconomic toll of flood disasters, which under climate change scenarios could reach up 
to USD 750 million in expected annual damages. This would notably hamper Bosnia and 
Herzegovina's socioeconomic progress. Avoiding such losses in sectors like agriculture and 
hydropower would be particularly significant.  
31. 
The project’s interventions offer detailed, sector-specific climate risk information, which 
is critical for strategic planning and operational optimization in sectors like hydropower. This 
targeted approach not only ensures the sustainable development of the hydropower sector but 
also facilitates a shift toward clean energy, contributing to greenhouse gas reduction. Similarly, 
safeguarding crucial infrastructure like roads and bridges will bolster economic development 
and community functionality. This, in turn, allows the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina 
government to allocate public funds more effectively, directing them toward socially significant 
initiatives rather than disaster recovery.   
32. 
In terms of social impact, the project is specifically designed to benefit vulnerable 
populations, with a focus on gender equality. By actively involving women – who are 
disproportionately affected by disasters – in decision-making processes, the project aims to 
counter existing gender disparities in employment and social roles in Bosnia and Herzegovina. 
33. 
Environmentally, the project deploys EbA strategies and nature-based solutions like 
riparian plantings and agro-forestry. These measures serve multiple purposes: they enhance 
water retention, regulate hydrological flows, mitigate natural hazards like flooding and soil 
erosion, and provide habitat preservation and reforestation benefits. In the long run, these 
interventions will significantly boost the country's environmental resilience to climate-induced 
flood disasters, thereby aiding in the protection of valuable natural assets such as land and 
forest.  
34. 
The Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS) identifies five priority sectors for 
national development planning: agriculture, food security, water, health, disaster risk reduction, 
and energy. The potential benefit-cost of return on investment could be up to 500:1 for flood-
prone areas and 2500:1 for heat waves in urban centers (Teisberg and Weiher,2008). The 
proposal could explore these benefits in a multi-hazard context for flood-related hazards and 
linkages to the other hazards of the GFCS priority sectors. The proposal would benefit from a 
clearer articulation and demonstration of health benefits from flood-related risk management. 
The proposal could benefit from more detailed elaboration of how these interventions could 
lead to health benefits from basic hydro-meteorological forecasting and impact-based 
forecasting. It is recommended that UNDP should consider laying the foundation for the 
establishment of the infrastructure and tools for multi-sectoral forecasts in the other four GFCS 
priority sectors through the GFCS user interface platform. We also proposed the multi-hazards 
forecasting system could follow overall value chain of risk information sectoral risk 
management. 
3.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
                             Scale: High 
35. 
Bosnia and Herzegovina is a middle-income nation with approximately 3.5 million 
residents. It is still grappling with the devastating effects of the 1992–1995 war, which 
decimated its human, economic, and social resources. These challenges are exacerbated by an 
ongoing transition toward a market economy that demands structural reforms and enhanced 
governance. Bosnia and Herzegovina's economy is in a transitional phase, heavily reliant on 
exports such as metals, energy, textiles and furniture, as well as on remittances and foreign aid. 
Poor governmental coordination and excessive bureaucracy have stifled economic growth and 
deterred foreign investment, making the economy one of the least competitive in the region.

=== PAGE 132 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 132 
 
 
36. 
Socially, poverty remains high, with 16.9 per cent of the population living below the 
poverty line as of 2015. While the Human Development Index showed improvement between 
2000 and 2019, the gains were significantly offset when adjusted for inequality. Additionally, 
the country's Gender Inequality Index ranks it 38th out of 162 countries. Climate change has 
further strained the slow post-war economic recovery, particularly impacting sectors like 
agriculture and hydropower due to increasing flood events.  
37. 
Vulnerable groups, including the rural poor and displaced individuals, have been most 
affected by flooding disasters. A lack of institutional capacity exacerbates these issues, with 
fragmented governance and poor communication hampering effective FRM. Key gaps in 
institutional capacity include insufficient hydrometric monitoring, outdated flood risk maps, 
and limited technical and financial resources for modern FRM strategies.  
38. 
The project aims to address these institutional challenges by implementing Flash Flood 
Early Warning Systems (FFEWS) based on clearly defined roles and responsibilities, creating 
protocols for effective FFEWS and FRM coordination, and employing state-of-the-art 
technologies and training to build capacity.  
3.5 
Country ownership 
                                                                  Scale: High 
39. 
The project demonstrates a strong national ownership, and has established the 
appropriate/relevant systems and processes (political, institutional and legal frameworks) for 
implementing its commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate 
Change. This includes the adoption of its intended nationally determined contributions under 
the Paris Agreement, for the period until 2030, in October 2015, and the development of its 
nationally determined contribution  in November 2020. The nationally determined contribution 
provides guidance on the country’s climate change adaptation and contribution to climate 
change mitigation. It identified the following as the country’s most vulnerable/priority sectors 
to climate change, for intervention: agriculture, water resources, forestry, energy, tourism, 
biodiversity and sensitive ecosystems, and human health, with the two most vulnerable sectors 
being agriculture and water resource management. It outlines the role of detailed planning of 
short, medium and long-term adaptation measures, development of climate risk financing 
mechanisms, and strengthening the responsible institutions in monitoring, analysis and 
modelling of climate risk.  The proposal is also aligned with the United Nations Sustainable 
Development Goals (in particular target 13) and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk 
Reduction (in particular target G). 
40. 
The proposal is developed based on these national strategies and the application of 
international recommendations. Strong country ownership is also demonstrated by significant 
government and local institution co-financing commitments. Co-financing support for the 
project will be provided by the Government of Republika Srpska through a European 
Investment Bank (EIB) loan of USD 21.5 million for structural measures and additionally 
through USD 36.0 million of the public funds to be provided by the governments of both entities 
composing the country – the Governments of the Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina and 
Republika Srpska for co-financing of EbA measures, the O&M of structural measures, 
hydrometric equipment, and modelling and FFEWS. 
3.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
                                                Scale: Medium 
41. 
The total budget for the proposed project is USD 72.69 million, of which GCF funding 
makes up 19.8 per cent, and co-financing accounts for the remaining 80.2 per cent. The 
Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina is expected to contribute 42 per cent or USD 30.74 
million, while EIB will provide 29 per cent in the form of senior loans. Despite facing budgetary 
and economic challenges, the substantial investment by the Government of Bosnia and

=== PAGE 133 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 133 
 
 
Herzegovina underscores its commitment to the initiative and, more importantly, to its social 
responsibility to provide an FFEWS as an essential service to the population. 
42. 
The GCF contribution is projected to mobilize an additional USD 58.29 million from the 
public and private sectors. Both project partners and beneficiaries are expected to bear the 
O&M costs related to all the project-supported infrastructure and hydrological equipment. 
While the project aims to produce significant public benefits, it is also designed to engage the 
public sector in limited cost-recovery measures for the ongoing operation and maintenance of 
the monitoring and early warning systems.   
43. 
Based on a detailed economic analysis (annex 3), the project estimated an economic 
internal rate of return (EIRR) of 19.3 per cent, and economic net present value (ENPV) of USD 
44.92 million. The results were calculated over a 20-year time frame at the economic discount 
rate of 10 per cent. The economic analysis of the project was carried out in accordance with the 
GCF Economic and Financial Analysis (EFA) Guidelines, and was conducted at the national level 
and then for each river sub-basin included in the proposed investment project. The results of 
the EFA suggest that the proposed investments can be justified from the economic point of view 
as obtained measures, ENPV and EIRR ensure the economic viability of proposed investments 
(e.g. the ENPV is positive, and the EIRR is higher than the chosen economic discount rate). 
Furthermore, the results of the EFA suggest that the proposed investment will be beneficial to 
the entire economy as the benefits of the proposed interventions will outweigh the investment 
costs.  
IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
44. 
Project background. The project will upgrade and expand the coverage of the 
hydrometric network and develop an integrated impact-based centralized and community-
based flood forecasting and early warning system. The project will also implement structural 
and non-structural flood risk reduction measures and nature-based solutions and strengthen 
institutional capacity for flood risk management. In addition, it proposes to scale up flood 
protection measures through improved national and local investment frameworks. Some of the 
expected environmental co-benefits are water retention functions from riparian plantings and 
agro-forestry and reduced erosion. Social co-benefits of the proposal are improved resilience of 
communities’ physical assets and protection of people from major flooding hazards. 
45. 
Environmental and social risk category. The confirmed and assigned environmental 
and social risk category of the project is equivalent to GCF’s medium/category B. It is within the 
accreditation level of the accredited entity (AE). This is rationalized by structural and non-
structural flood reduction interventions that will be promoted by the project and are likely to 
have moderate risks which are generally reversible and site-specific which can also be readily 
managed with mitigation measures. Activity 2.2 non-structural measures include gabion walls, 
torrent and wicker barriers, and planting forest strips among others. Activity 3.3 structural 
measures include rehabilitation of river embankments, and canals and establishing dykes. 
Additionally, the associated risks and impacts of activities such as upgrading and expansion of 
the coverage of the hydrometric system and capacity-building such as training and development 
of guidelines, plans and frameworks are expected to be low and minimal.  
46. 
Safeguard instruments. The AE provided an environmental and social assessment 
report (ESAR) and environmental and social and management framework (ESMF) with the 
funding proposal as the safeguards document. The document provides an overview of the 
national context for environmental and social risk assessment, potential environmental and 
social risks likely to be associated with the project activities, including medium risk site-specific

=== PAGE 134 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 134 
 
 
environment and social studies that will be undertaken during the project’s implementation. 
Environmental impact assessment will be required for completely new facilities built by the 
project according to national regulations, however, other proposed activities such as the 
reconstruction of existing dykes and inlets fall within exemptions provided to the water 
authorities which will be confirmed prior to commencing implementation. 
4.1.1. 
Compliance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards.  
47. 
ESS 1) Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and 
impacts. The ESAR was prepared based on the environmental and social risks identified 
through the screening of activities using the AE’s procedure. It includes an assessment of the 
significance level of each risk that was identified under each activity. The ESMF provides an 
outline of a range of mitigation measures that will be undertaken when implementing the 
project to manage risks and impacts and the site-specific environmental and social assessments 
and management plans that will be prepared for moderate risk structural and non-structural 
flood protection measures, for which outlines of the documents are annexed into the ESAR and 
ESMF. Baseline environmental and socioeconomic information about Bosnia and Herzegovina is 
also presented in the ESAR and ESMF. The main outcomes of the environmental and social 
assessment undertaken, including the key risks and impacts and proposed measures to be taken 
to avoid, minimize and mitigate them are summarized below. A gap analysis of the AE’s 
environmental and social standards, those of EIB, a co-financier and national policies, legislation 
and regulations is included in the document. The AE is recommended to include GCF standards 
in the analysis.  
48. 
ESS 2) Labour and working conditions. There is a risk that work sites may flood 
during the implementation of flood protection measures. This will be mitigated by timing the 
works appropriately and monitoring of upstream rainfall and river levels prior and during the 
works. Contracts and working conditions for personnel working on the project will be in line 
with relevant national legislation and in compliance with UNDP Social and Environmental 
Standard 7. Appropriate personal protective equipment will be provided to personnel who need 
it. 
49. 
The AE is advised to ensure that vendors are evaluated during the procurement process 
and ensure inclusion of requirements and standards concerning irregular working conditions 
for hydrological, meteorological and precipitation stations’ solar components that may be 
procured by the project.  
50. 
ESS 3) Resource efficiency and pollution prevention. It is anticipated that most 
materials for construction purposes, particularly rocks, will come from quarries near the work 
sites. The project will ensure proper handling of materials, and where possible, recycling and 
reuse of any local materials may be considered. The key waste streams likely to be generated 
are residual sediment and construction wastes which will be minimized, have designated areas 
for temporary management and disposed of according to local requirements. Potential erosion 
and sediment movement may also occur during works that involve physical interventions. To 
avoid significantly increased sediment discharges and erosion, the AE will ensure project 
activities are not carried out during periods of rainfall. There is a small chance of water 
pollution from oil, grease and fuel spills, and other materials from vehicles working on site.  
51. 
ESS 4) Community health, safety and security. The project is expected to involve 
minor construction work impacts for the upgrade and expansion of the coverage of the 
hydrometric network. Other construction-related risks include noise and dust from heavy 
construction machinery and haul trucks. Some of the proposed mitigation measures are 
sourcing water for dust suppression during the implementation of activities that generate dust 
and restricting use of machinery and vehicles to normal working hours during the day.

=== PAGE 135 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 135 
 
 
52. 
Additionally, there is the possibility of mines and unexploded ordnance (UXO) 
disturbance by earthworks for which the project will update the magnetometry survey to 
confirm the presence or absence of UXO and the contractor will be required to detail procedures 
to safely handle and dispose of UXO if any UXO are encountered during works. 
53. 
Conflict sensitivity analysis is included in the ESAR and ESMF which assesses the conflict 
dynamics in the country and the project activities’ potential to cause conflict. Most risks are 
classified as low and low–medium. The AE concludes that the probability of conflicts occurring 
or being exacerbated due to the project interventions is slight. Recommended conflict 
mitigation measures include stakeholder engagement and the use of grievance mechanisms 
available for the project. 
54. 
ESS 5) Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. Activities involving physical 
interventions may result in economic displacement from construction activities that alter 
people’s ability to move or access resources. The AE will be required to ensure that when this 
standard is triggered, plans for the restoration of livelihoods accompany the site-specific 
environmental and social assessments and management plans whether economic displacement 
is temporary or permanent. The AE is recommended to pay particular attention to related 
impacts on internally displaced persons who may be more vulnerable. The project will not 
result in physical displacement. Sites for physical interventions have been selected only where 
land tenure rests with the government, that is public land.  
55. 
ESS 6) Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resources. Implementation of structural and non-structural measures proposed by the project 
may potentially cause habitat loss and disturbance of fauna. This will be mitigated by 
revegetation where vegetation is removed by the project, avoiding in-stream works during the 
spawning season and minimizing water turbidity during works on waterways. Disturbed areas 
will be revegetated using native and locally endemic species that have high habitat value. Native 
and locally endemic species will be used to revegetate to mitigate against invasive species. A 
biodiversity action plan may be prepared for the Krupa River site in case there are likely 
impacts to the Hutovo Blato Ramsar site. 
56. 
GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7) Indigenous Peoples. The AE confirmed 
there were no Indigenous Peoples or internally displaced persons affected by the project and 
that the EIB, which has a related investment and uses a similar Indigenous Peoples safeguard, 
had come to the same conclusion. Accordingly, the AE concluded that the Indigenous Peoples 
Policy, ESS 7 and its own Social and Environmental Standard 6 were not triggered.   
57. 
ESS 8) Cultural heritage. Screening for the project indicated that cultural physical 
resources were unlikely to be at risk as a result of the project’s implementation. Nonetheless, 
there is the possibility that unexpected cultural heritage items could be discovered during 
works. A chance finds procedure is annexed into the ESAR and ESMF for this eventuality. 
58. 
Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment (SEAH) safeguarding. 
The AE has identified the project’s moderate SEAH risks related to the construction work for 
implementing new ecosystem-based flood risk reduction and climate change adaptation 
methods, and when conducting regular project information and training sessions. The project’s 
ESAR and ESMF has triggered AE’s relevant policies, safeguard standards and programming 
principles on the prevention and elimination of SEAH. The AE will implement a SEAH Action 
Plan to address the potential risks and impacts of SEAH and will require the project’s 
contractors, suppliers and partners to adhere to zero tolerance for SEAH. The AE will terminate 
contractual arrangements with those who cannot conduct and commit to taking adequate action 
if faced with SEAH allegations. As part of the project’s SEAH Action Plan, the project will ensure 
the availability of an effective GRM with multiple channels to initiate a complaint, including 
confidential reporting with safe and ethical documenting of SEAH cases. The project will map 
out SEAH prevention and response actors in the project’s adjoining communities, develop

=== PAGE 136 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 136 
 
 
training materials and organize SEAH training for project workers and the local community, 
clearly define SEAH requirements and expectations in the bid documents, and undertake 
regular monitoring and evaluation of progress on SEAH prevention and response activities, 
including reassessment of risks as appropriate. In the event of gender-based violence/SEAH 
complaints, the project’s Gender Advisor will serve as the SEAH focal point to connect the SEAH 
survivor with relevant service providers. 
59. 
 Implementation arrangements. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will ensure 
timely remedial actions are taken by the contractor where necessary. The PMU will have an 
International Safeguards Officer responsible for overseeing and coordinating the 
implementation of safeguards across the project and ensuring consistent application of 
measures by the separate water agencies/contractors involved in the project. This officer will 
ensure that relevant standards are met. The Safeguards Officer will be supported by field 
officers who will be responsible for day-to-day inspection to ensure that the required 
safeguards are being implemented and monitored at the site level. Delivery organizations have 
the responsibility for ensuring that relevant employees and contractors are aware of the 
environmental and social safeguards requirements. Personnel working on the project will 
receive training in environmental, health and safety requirements. UNDP, as the AE, will 
maintain oversight on all matters related to environmental and social safeguards. A budget is 
included in the ESAR and ESMF with a breakdown of how resources allocated for the 
implementation of safeguards matters will be utilized.  
60. 
The AE is recommended to assess the capacity of the project’s delivery organizations 
and provide interventions that strengthen the relevant capacities that are required to undertake 
responsibilities related to environmental and social safeguards matters. 
61. 
Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure. Consultations on the project 
were undertaken during the design phase with a wide range of stakeholders, including relevant 
national authorities, industry groups, civil society organizations, and individual community 
members. A report on stakeholder consultations undertaken is presented in a stakeholder 
engagement plan. The AE and its partners will develop and release updates on the project on a 
regular basis to provide interested stakeholders with information on project status and 
circulate this through various media. Stakeholder engagement activities that will support 
project outcomes are listed in the plan according to project activities and stakeholders 
identified, their roles and means of participation.  
62. 
Grievance redress mechanism (GRM). Grievances can be registered by telephone via 
the project-level grievance redress mechanism that will be publicized and maintained 
throughout the project to serve as a point of contact for enquiries and grievances concerning the 
project. Complaints will be recorded on a register, including details of the response and 
investigation undertaken and actions taken to address the matter. The PMU Safeguards Officer 
will be responsible for the functioning of the grievance mechanism of the project. The process 
for responding to grievances is outlined in the ESAR and ESMF. Furthermore, details of how the 
AE’s institutional-level grievance mechanism and the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism can 
be contacted have been included in the document. 
4.2 
Gender policy  
63. 
The AE has provided a gender assessment and Gender Action Plan therefore complies 
with the requirements of the GCF’s Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan.  
64. 
The gender assessment describes the enabling environment and gender issues at the 
national level and indicates the existence of a national policy environment, supportive 
legislation and practices to promote gender equality. It also identifies gendered challenges and 
barriers that leave women more vulnerable to climate-related disasters. Despite a strong policy

=== PAGE 137 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 137 
 
 
and legal framework supporting gender equality and women are still largely excluded from 
political and decision-making processes; they have disproportionately limited access to 
employment and income, and many are subject to violence. There is particular concern with 
respect to women internally displaced persons, persons with disabilities, older persons and 
Roma women in Bosnia and Herzegovina, who are even more severely disadvantaged and 
marginalized. And institutional capacity to implement a normative framework for gender 
equality remains a serious challenge. The greatest obstacles to broader development and 
empowerment of women in rural areas are related to property rights, access to public services, 
finance, training, advisory services and mentorship, and limited time to engage in activities 
outside of their homes. Limited opportunities for employment and self-employment are 
perpetuated by low levels of understanding of opportunities coupled with a lack of access to 
information and training. A lack of affordable childcare and pre-schools in rural and smaller 
settlements is one of the central obstacles to women’s participation in trainings, entrepreneurial 
activities, and consequently, employment. There is also a limited number of women associations 
and cooperatives which exacerbates the lack of access to relevant information and services. 
Moreover, discrimination against Roma women is evident from the level of their formal 
employment. This discrimination, especially toward Roma girls, is also reflected in their right to 
education. Every second woman has experienced some form of gender-based violence from the 
age of 15, while every fifth woman has most likely experienced this over the past 12 months. 
There are several vulnerabilities that increase the risk to women from natural disasters, and 
based on experiences following the floods in 2014, the Republika Srpska Gender Centre has 
developed a Gender Action Plan for natural disasters and has incorporated measures into it to 
address these challenges. 
65. 
The AE has provided a programme-level Gender Action Plan, which includes activities, 
indicators, targets, budget, timeline and gender expertise to ensure that gender issues are 
incorporated into the project and GAP activities. While an initial gender analysis was conducted, 
further analysis will be undertaken to ensure that the baseline data set is sex- and age-
disaggregated and adequate for assessment of the project’s gender impacts. The gender 
analysiswill assess gender-specific vulnerabilities to flood risks through gender-responsive 
socioeconomic data collection, and ensure women’s participation and decision-making as well 
as information access and warning dissemination services. Further, the project will ensure 
women are involved in the processes of capacity-building for climate-induced hazard risk 
assessment within the FRM at all levels, but also in the development of public–private 
collaboration on building resilience by climate risk finance mechanism. Men and women of 
different age groups, as well as those from vulnerable communities will be involved in planning 
and implementation. Community consultancy groups with at least 40 per cent representation of 
women will be established. The project will ensure that information on hazards, their character, 
etc., reaches both women and men through appropriately focused channels. It will also tailor a 
warning to the gender-differentiated needs and capabilities of specific population groups, such 
as children, older persons, internally displaced persons, the sick and persons with disabilities.  
The project will also ensure that women have access to information and livelihood-generating 
activities. The project will strengthen social capital through the creation of community groups 
for planning for, safeguarding from and mitigating against adverse effects of natural disasters 
with at least 40 per cent women’s participation. Additionally, the project will conduct gender-
mainstreaming trainings and build capacity of national stakeholders targeted by the project and 
responsible for various disaster risk reduction functions. The project will ensure at least 30 per 
cent representation and active participation of women in project stakeholder consultations, and 
local and national decision-making bodies established and/or facilitated by the project. The 
project will secure participation of the gender institutional mechanism representatives in the 
consultations and decision-making. In developing an investment framework for climate-induced 
flood risk reduction and management, the project will include representatives from the gender 
mechanisms. A gender-responsive strategy and gender-sensitive tools for private sector 
engagement in long-term climate-proof risk management will be developed along with the

=== PAGE 138 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 138 
 
 
development of risk financing and transfer mechanisms based on detailed gender-sensitive 
assessment on socioeconomic risk, damages and losses. Multi-year climate resilient municipal 
investment plans and gender-responsive community preparedness plans will be developed in 
selected municipalities (10–12) and in one canton. The project will ensure men and women (40 
per cent) are represented in municipal investment planning process and that they actively 
participate in the formulation of community preparedness plans in selected municipalities and 
that the needs of women and other vulnerable (older persons, persons with disabilities, 
children, Roma) groups are incorporated into any plans developed. 
66. 
Finally, the project will implement climate-proof structural flood risk reduction and 
anti-erosion interventions in the Vrbas, Una-Sana, Bosna and Drina River Basins. Both, women 
(40 per cent) and men, and other vulnerable groups (older persons, persons with disabilities, 
children, Roma) will be included in processes of stakeholders’ consultation related to the 
implementation of climate-proof flood risk measures. The project will ensure that specific ways 
in which flood risk and erosion impact women are analysed and considered. Ensure that women 
and vulnerable group members can equally benefit from technical assistance. The AE will aim to 
ensure at least 50 per cent of women participants have benefited from the employment and 
income-making opportunities associated with implementing these measures. 
67. 
The project will engage a gender expert and the project implementation team, staff 
members will be trained on gender mainstreaming and on the social vulnerability approach. 
Project team leaders will ensure that all specialists are briefed on their responsibilities in 
relation to the Gender Action Plan upon mobilization. 
68. 
The AE is recommended to consistently mainstream gender throughout the project as a 
means to meet the needs of women and other vulnerable people. There is great potential to 
consider gender issues significantly and sufficiently in all flood risk information and warning 
messages, in amendments to all sectoral planning documents and feasibility studies on 
microinsurance and regional disaster risk insurance programmes and catastrophe bonds. 
Further concerns remain with the lack of clarity on indicators that will be used to measure 
women’s access and use of products and mechanisms such as risk financing, transfer 
mechanism products and responsible financial products with corresponding targets. These 
considerations, if taken seriously, will significantly contribute to women’s empowerment. 
4.3 
Risks  
4.3.1. 
Overall programme assessment (medium-high risk)  
69. 
GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 14.4 million, which will be invested in flood 
risk management by strengthening the use of climate information, flood forecasting, early 
warning and emergency response systems to enhance adaptive capacity and resilience of at-risk 
communities. The Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina will provide a senior loan of 
approximately USD 21.1 million which is from EIB. The government will also provide a grant 
along with other partners which will be a total of approximately USD 37.2 million. While tThe 
co-financing accounts for 80 per cent of the total budget of this project, some of the details 
around the co-financing will be further negotiated at the FAA stage 
70. 
The AE is requesting full grant support for this project. As the government will take out 
a loan from EIB to finance structural measures, the grant from GCF will finance timely 
complementary interventions such as non-structural measures and technical assistance. 
4.3.2. 
Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme 
(Low risk)

=== PAGE 139 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 139 
 
 
71. 
UNDP is the AE and also the executing entity (EE) for the project. The project will be 
implemented by UNDP following UNDP’s Direct Implementation Modality (DIM). The AE has 
been operating in the country since 1996. As the funding proposal involves different 
government entities with the complex structure, UNDP's coordination role will be indispensable 
for successful project implementation. 
4.3.3. 
Programme-specific execution risks (medium-high risk) 
72. 
Complex governance structure. The AE has identified several governance-related risks 
in the funding proposal. The funded activities include a number of policy and regulation 
updates. For example, activity 2.1 is on financing the development of countrywide flood zoning 
policy and regulation. The governments of the two entities need to confirm their acceptance of 
the policy and regulations once they are updated. For activity 3.1, the project will facilitate the 
changes to the current regulations in the field of insurance. The relevant legal and regulatory 
provisions require adoption by the governments to enable the implementation of flood-risk 
financing instruments. The fragmented institutional structure of the governments and a lack of 
political consensus may hinder the process and limit the policy effectiveness even after the 
updates/changes. The AE will leverage the existing relationship and relevant experience from 
past projects to mitigate such risks.  
73. 
Co-financing risk. the ratio of GCF funding to co-financing is 1:4. The sizable co-
financing is critical for the funded activities, especially for activities which are implemented 
through blended funds from GCF and co-financers such as activity 2.1. If the disbursement of co-
financing is delayed or reduced, it will hamper project implementation. To mitigate this risk, it is 
recommended that the term sheet include a proportionate disbursement between GCF and co-
financing with a specific ratio which would be in line with the implementation plan each year. 
However, the details for disbursement and how to address the shortfall in co-financing will be 
further negotiated at the FAA stage.  
74. 
As part of risk financing and as a risk transfer measure, the project will provide technical 
assistance and training to financial institutions for the development of lending products and the 
use of climate risk information into their lending process to provide loans to climate-resilient 
enterprises in Bosnia and Herzegovina. However, technical assessment and training will not 
automatically lead to the provision of loans to enterprises unless there are incentives for the 
financial institutions. A specific target or indicator to monitor the number of loans to be 
provided and/or engagement of financial institutions will enhance the impact of the funding 
proposal.   
4.3.4. 
Compliance risk (medium risk)   
75. 
UNDP confirmed that it has conducted a risk assessment in relation to money-
laundering and the financing of terrorism risks. The probability of occurrence of a prohibited 
practice event has been assessed as low, whereas the impact severity of occurrence has been 
assessed as medium. 
76. 
UNDP confirmed that it has also conducted a risk assessment in relation to sanction 
risks. The probability of occurrence of a prohibited practice event has been assessed as low, 
whereas the impact severity of occurrence has been assessed as medium. 
77. 
UNDP noted that this project will be implemented under the DIM and will be guided by 
UNDP’s policy framework. UNDP, by virtue of being an organ of the United Nations and 
therefore being part of the same entity, is also beholden to the principles on anti-money-
laundering/countering the financing of terrorism.  
78. 
UNDP systematically screens all entities being considered to be contracted by or partner 
with UNDP against the Consolidated United Nations Security Council List. This is built into the

=== PAGE 140 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 140 
 
 
capacity-screening tools for partners, as well as in the SOPs for vendor management. UNDP also 
secures contractual representations from its national implementing partners that address risks 
of the funds received for implementation of projects being used to support individuals or 
entities associated with terrorism and recipients of any amounts provided by UNDP hereunder 
appearing on the Consolidated List. 
79. 
These internal controls are further supplemented by internal controls to mitigate the 
risks, including due diligence requirements for partners and private entities which UNDP 
intends to engage with. When developing a new project, UNDP ensures that risks are fully 
identified and considered in the project design and processes and that adequate and effective 
measures to prevent, monitor and address such risks are put in place.  
80. 
UNDP confirmed that there will be no third parties involved in the delivery of the GCF 
funds (such as the implementing party or responsible parties). UNDP will deliver all activities 
through the UNDP procurement process/UNDP-approved vendors. Therefore, the due diligence 
process for third parties was not triggered at the project proposal stage. However, UNDP has 
carried out the first level due diligence assessment of all co-financiers, including public and 
private co-financing entities, which will be implementing their respective co-financed activities 
in the framework of the project. The assessments have been carried out in line with UNDP’s 
policies and risk assessment tools.  
81. 
UNDP also referred to the Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers (HACT) applied by 
UNDP, which represents a common operational (harmonized) framework for transferring cash 
to government and non-governmental partners (both implementing partners and responsible 
parties). This project initially does not envisage engaging responsible parties. However, in the 
event that responsible parties are engaged in the future for project implementation, the HACT 
framework will be applied by UNDP.  
82. 
The common objective of the HACT framework is to support the closer alignment of 
development aid with national priorities and to strengthen national capacities for management 
and accountability. It serves as a set of procedures on requesting, disbursing, providing 
assurance, and reporting on funds to effectively manage risks, reduce transaction costs and 
promote sustainable development in a coordinated manner. Cash transfers over a certain 
amount (above the threshold of USD 150,000 in a programme cycle, or otherwise in all 
circumstances where UNDP considers the operating environment high risk or has no prior 
experience with the partner) are presumed to present a material risk to UNDP and will require a 
HACT micro-assessment and risk rating by qualified third party service providers. Spot checks, 
programmatic output verification and auditing requirements are then determined and carried 
out at a frequency determined in line with the risk rating. 
83. 
UNDP confirmed that cash, vouchers, commodities or other items of value will not be 
distributed or disbursed to any beneficiaries involved in the project, either directly or indirectly. 
84. 
UNDP confirmed that it has carried out first-level due diligence assessment of all co-
financiers. For public-sector partners (five entities), UNDP’s Partner Capacity Assessment Tool 
(PCAT) has been used. For the private sector co-financiers (three entities), UNDP’s Private 
Sector Risk Assessment Tool has been used in line with the UNDP Policy on Due Diligence and 
Partnerships with the Private Sector.  
85. 
None of the co-financiers are listed on the Consolidated List, the UNDP Vendor Sanctions 
List or the United Nations Global Marketplace (UNGM) Ineligibility List. Assessments have not 
found any evidence of fraud, corruption or violation of programming principles or standards by 
these co-financing entities. These due diligence assessments have not revealed any violations or 
concerns over the partners’ capacities to deliver their co-financed activities in the framework of 
the project. In addition, co-financiers are required to reconfirm their commitments to meet the 
requirements of the UNDP social and environmental safeguards policy and standards prior to 
the launch of the project implementation.

=== PAGE 141 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 141 
 
 
86. 
UNDP confirmed that it has conducted a risk assessment in relation to prohibited 
practices risks. The probability of occurrence of a prohibited practice event has been assessed 
as low, whereas the impact severity of occurrence has been assessed as medium. 
87. 
UNDP stated that it applies a zero-tolerance policy in relation to fraud and corruption. 
The guiding principles of UNDP's commitment to prevent, identify and address all acts of fraud 
and corruption have been laid out in the UNDP Policy Against Fraud and Corrupt Practices (FCP 
Policy), which applies to all activities and operations of UNDP, including projects and 
programmes. The fundamental principles of UNDP's FCP Policy serve as the basis for and are 
integrated into UNDP's policy frameworks in relation to procurement, financial management, 
internal control and accountability, and staff rules and regulations.  
88. 
Under the terms of the FCP Policy and related policies, UNDP has a commitment, when 
developing a new programme or project, to ensure that risks related to fraud and corrupt 
practices are fully identified and considered in the programme/project design and processes 
and that adequate and effective measures to mitigate such risks are put in place. In this light, the 
processes and requirements for UNDP's PCAT require UNDP to carry out a thorough due 
diligence of the risks in relation to the programme/project it intends to engage in, as well as in 
relation to the potential partners who will be involved in the implementation of such 
programmes/projects.  
89. 
Such capacity assessments include, among other things, a screening of all entities being 
considered to be contracted by or partner with UNDP against the Consolidated List (to which 
UNDP, by virtue of being an organ of the United Nations and therefore being part of the same 
entity, is also beholden), an assessment of any history of fraud, corruption or other fraudulent 
practices and/or potential conflicts of interest. Based on this capacity assessment, UNDP then 
builds an internal control component into the design of the project/programme and ensures 
that its financial management contains adequate safeguards to prevent, monitor and address 
any risks and acts of fraud, corruption or anti-money-laundering/countering the financing of 
terrorism that may be identified. 
90. 
UNDP confirmed that the project has developed a grievance redress mechanism to deal 
with any complaints and issues that may arise because of the project. The GRM has been 
designed to ensure that an individual and/or group are not financially impacted by the process 
of making a complaint and/or grievance. The GRM complies with safeguard procedures of 
Bosnia and Herzegovina and UNDP. Furthermore, the GRM allows individuals and/or groups to 
also file a complaint with the Office of Anticorruption and Integrity within the UNDP or utilize 
the GCF independent Redress Mechanism should they have any concerns as to corruption, 
unethical behaviour or where they believe their complaint or grievance has not been adequately 
addressed. 
91. 
Compliance risk rating: The Secretariat has conducted a review of the project in 
accordance with relevant Board-approved policies and does not find any material issue or 
deviation with respect to compliance issues. Based on available information for this funding 
proposal, the Secretariat has determined a compliance risk rating of medium and has no 
objection to this proposal.

  As of 7 pm on 2 October 2023, there remains a divergence of views between the GCF 
Secretariat and the AE concerning how co-financing would come through. Co-financing accounts 
for more than 80% of total funding, therefore clear mechanism and undertaking by the AE on 
how the co-financing would come through is important for the implementation per the outlined 
targets and implementation plan. Despite numerous rounds of reviews, the AE has not yet been 
amenable to including a covenant on co-financing. Given the GCF to co-financing ratio of 1:4, the 
Secretariat has been hesitant to advance the co-financing covenant discussions to the FAA stage. 
However, since no agreement on the co-financing covenants has been reached, we have to defer 
relevant clauses on co-financing to the FAA stage on a condition that the language of this 
covenant to set out a mechanism for the co-financing to be contributed by all the co-financiers 
listed in the FP in line with the targets, total amount, Budget and Workplan (per Annex 1) as 
outlined in the FP. We propose to include the proportionality of the GCF proceeds disbursed 
each year with the proceeds of co-financiers, inline with the Budget.

103.

became effective on 23 November 2016. The Accredited Entity’s first term of accreditation 
expired on 22 November 2021. The Accredited Entity was re-accredited by the Board pursuant

obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project. 
105. 
 The proposed project will be implemented in Bosnia and Herzegovina, country in which 
GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF 
is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be 
further assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities 
and a background note to the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina on 11 May 2016. The 
latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Bosnia Herzegovina 
on 7 February 2018. 
106. 
The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity 
Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress 
activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with 
relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the 
GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and 
expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.
