Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP216 
Proposal name: 
Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in Bosnia 
and Herzegovina 
Accredited entity: 
United Nations Development Programme 
Country/(ies): 
Bosnia and Herzegovina 
Project/programme size: 
Medium 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Overview   
1. 
In its assessment, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) is guided by 
decision B.33/12 adopted by the Board, which explains the principles for demonstrating the 
impact potential of GCF-supported activities as well as other appraisal and assessment guidance 
in considering these investment criteria and as endorsed by the Board. 
2. 
The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) has assessed the scientific 
literature regarding the impacts of climate change on Bosnia and Herzegovina and has reached 
several important conclusions. According to its findings, the country (and the wider region) is 
susceptible to a variety of adverse effects as a result of global warming.1 
3. 
One of the key impacts of climate change identified by the IPCC in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina is the alteration of precipitation patterns. It is projected that the country will 
experience an overall decrease in annual rainfall, coupled with an increase in the intensity of 
extreme weather events, such as heavy rainfalls and floods. This alteration in precipitation 
patterns is likely to exacerbate water-related issues, including water scarcity, droughts and 
potential threats to agriculture. 
4. 
Furthermore, rising temperatures are identified as a significant concern for Bosnia and 
Herzegovina and the region. The IPCC anticipates an increase in average temperatures, resulting 
in more frequent and prolonged heatwaves. This rise in temperature could have detrimental 
effects on ecosystems, agriculture and public health, notably increasing the risk of forest fires 
and heat-related illnesses. 
5. 
As such, recent research has indicated that climate change will likely exacerbate flood 
risks in Bosnia and Herzegovina. Rising temperatures have led to more intense rainfall and 
increased snowmelt, subsequently overwhelming drainage systems and increasing river flow. 
As Bosnia and Herzegovina is already prone to flooding due to its mountainous terrain and 
complex river network, the impacts of climate change will be particularly severe. Moreover, 
flooding poses a significant threat to the country’s infrastructure, agriculture and public health. 
Increased frequency and intensity of floods may damage roads, bridges and buildings, resulting 
 
1 See World Bank. 2021. Climate Risk Country Profile, Bosnia and Herzegovina. Available at 
https://climateknowledgeportal.worldbank.org/sites/default/files/2021-07/15914-
WB_Bosnia%20Country%20Profile-WEB%20%281%29.pdf (accessed 15 September 2023). See also Government of 
Bosnia and Herzegovina. 2021. National Adaptation Plan – NAP with proposed measures, specifically chapter 7. 
Available at https://unfccc.int/sites/default/files/resource/NAP-Bosnia-and-Herzegovina%20.pdf (accessed 13 
August 2023).

=== PAGE 146 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 146 
 
 
 
 
in economic losses. Agriculture, a vital sector for the country’s economy, will also be adversely 
affected by floods, leading to a decline in productivity and potential food shortages. 
Furthermore, flooding increases the risk of waterborne diseases, posing a threat to public health 
and well-being. 
6. 
Considering these developments, the IPCC emphasizes the importance of adapting to 
climate change in Bosnia and Herzegovina. It suggests implementing strategies to mitigate the 
identified risks, including the development of resilient infrastructure, the promotion of 
sustainable agricultural practices, and the enhancement of early warning systems for extreme 
weather events.   
7. 
The project titled “Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina” proposed by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) seeks to 
respond to this call for a strengthening of adaptive capacity in the country. The overall objective 
of the project is to establish a comprehensive and innovative flood risk management approach 
that is responsive to gender issues and climate change impacts. The project also seeks to 
enhance the capacity of institutions, both technically and financially, to implement long-term 
flood risk management strategies that incorporate a range of measures, including structural (i.e. 
physical construction) and non-structural (i.e. land-use planning and zoning) measures as well 
as ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA, working with nature and natural processes). Additionally, 
the project aims to improve the utilization of climate information, flood forecasting, early 
warning systems, and emergency response mechanisms to strengthen the adaptive capacity and 
resilience of vulnerable communities in Bosnia and Herzegovina. The project aligns with the 
Early Warnings for All Initiative which was announced by the United Nations Secretary-General 
in March 2022.  
8. 
The project is designed to scale up the Technology Transfer for Climate Resilient Flood 
Risk Management (Vrbas project) that UNDP implemented between 2014 and 2020 with 
funding from the Global Environment Facility, and that focused on introducing state-of-the-art 
climate-resilient flood management (including, but not limited to, an upgrade and rehabilitation 
of the hydrometric monitoring network and development of a flood forecasting and enhanced 
the existing early warning system) for the Vrbas River Basin.2 
1.2 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
            Scale: Medium 
9. 
The funding proposal highlights various anticipated adaptation impacts resulting from 
the proposed project across various GCF adaptation results areas. Specifically, the funding 
proposal references two main project outcomes: 
(a) 
Outcome 1: Climate informed gender-responsive impact-based Flood Forecasting and 
Early Warning System and increased generation and use of climate data to reduce 
vulnerability to flood related disasters; and 
(b) 
Outcome 2: Vulnerable communities in Bosnia and Herzegovina, including women, 
children, people with disabilities, and older persons, are better protected from and less 
exposed to climate induced flood risks through a combination of structural and non-
structural flood risk reduction solutions. 
 
2 That baseline project received a “Satisfactory” rating in the independent terminal evaluation report: “The project 
has achieved impressive results. The 638,600 ha VRB is under improved management for climate resilience through 
successful transfer of climate adaptation technology and advances to policy and regulatory frameworks. The strong 
emphasis on institutional strengthening has built an enhanced enabling environment for facilitating follow-up 
actions. The incremental benefits achieved through the GEF funding is confirmed through the replication that has 
occurred during the project implementation phase and the high potential and expected investments for upscaling” 
(annex 18, p. 90).

=== PAGE 147 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 147 
 
 
 
 
10. 
These outcomes are anticipated to be accomplished through three outputs, namely: (1) 
the implementation of a fully integrated impact-based FFEWS allows for prompt preparation 
and response; (2) the integration of non-structural flood risk reduction measures and nature-
based solutions in sectoral policies and plans, effectively contributing to the protection of 
people and livelihoods from climate-induced flood risk; and (3) the scaling-up of climate-proof 
flood protection measures through new and improved national and local investment 
frameworks increasing the resilience of the most vulnerable groups to climate-induced flooding. 
These outputs are organized into eleven distinct activities and 35 subactivities. 
11. 
In conjunction, the project interventions are expected to yield the following positive 
adaptation impacts in Bosnia and Herzegovina, as estimated below in relation to GCF core 
indicators and adaptation results areas: 
(a) 
799,445 direct and 1,977,244 indirect beneficiaries that benefit from reduced 
vulnerability to loss of life, property and critical infrastructure (core indicator 2); 
(b) 
22,516 direct and 741,339 indirect beneficiaries protected from extreme floods in those 
municipalities where interventions are implemented (adaptation results area 1 (ARA 
1));  
(c) 
77,929 direct and 1,995,257 indirect beneficiaries benefiting from improved health, 
well-being, food and water security as a result of enhanced access to timely early 
warning and climate information (ARA 2); 
(d) 
776,929 beneficiaries covered by new or improved early warning systems (ARA 
supplementary 2.4); 
(e) 
USD 501.8 million of household assets and USD 109 million of business assets made 
more resilient to the effects of climate change (ARA 3); 
(f) 
USD 83.5 million reduction in expected economic losses to households and USD 19 
million reduction in business assets (ARA 3 supplementary 3.1); 
(g) 
22,516 direct beneficiaries living in buildings that have increased climate resilience 
(ARA 3 supplementary 2.6); and 
(h) 
5,081 ha of land protected from extreme floods (ARA 4).  
12. 
The funding proposal contains a detailed description of the methodology adopted for 
the Geographic Information System-based risk and vulnerability modelling and mapping and 
cost–benefit options appraisal that forms the basis of the calculation of these anticipated 
adaptation impacts (annex E to annex 2). It also contains the results of the socioeconomic 
analysis across municipalities in Bosnia and Herzegovina (annex D to annex 2) under current as 
well as expected future climate change conditions, and an economic analysis comparing 
estimated costs and benefits of the project (annex 3a and 3b). However, the results of this 
modelling exercise need to be interpreted with caution. First, the iTAP found it somewhat 
challenging to reconstruct the stated adaptation impact targets of the project. The feasibility 
study (annex 2) refers to the calibration and validation of the underlying flood risk model but 
does not contain a comprehensive description of its results to gauge the degree of its precision 
and thus the confidence in the extent of future floods. Second, the estimated adaptation benefits 
are based on the detailed climate change flood hydrological and hydraulic modelling for the 
Vrbas River Basin that was conducted as part of the baseline project. In the absence of detailed 
hazard mapping for the other river basins, the overall modelling results for the entire proposed 
project region across five river basis assumes that the climate change scenarios and impact of 
proposed interventions will be similar for these other river basins (Una-Sana, Bosna, Neretva 
and Trebišnjica). While this may well be a reasonable assumption, the approach taken is overly 
simplistic and thus the results cannot be accepted with certainty without consideration of 
alternative scenarios or some insights related to land cover dynamics, among others, related to 
these other river basins.

=== PAGE 148 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 148 
 
 
 
 
13. 
Another methodological challenge is the choice of the Representative Concentration 
Pathway (RCP) scenario8.5 that is being utilized as the base scenario to inform the design of the 
proposed project interventions (as highlighted for example by the response of the accredited 
entity (AE) to the question-and-answer session (Q&A) with the iTAP). In the iTAP’s view, it 
would have been prudent to also model the design of project interventions based on alternative, 
and equally relevant scenarios such as RCP 4.5 or RCP 6 that include increasingly frequent and 
intense climate events for example (e.g. intense precipitation resulting in floods and landslides, 
heatwaves causing fast melting of snow which then trigger floods). These alternative scenarios 
are likely to have more significant negative impact on livelihoods and business operations over 
time and may require different interventions to support adaptation. The RCP 8.5 scenario (as a 
“high business-as-usual scenario”) generates a higher likelihood of large catastrophic events 
triggered by much more significant temperature increases towards the end of the century (akin 
to floods such as the 2014 flood in Bosnia and Herzegovina that represented a 1 in 500-year 
event). The RCP 8.5 scenario may result in higher costs for the adaptation measures at the 
expense of other important interventions. It may also create a false sense of security among 
stakeholders (e.g. communities, businesses, insurance) that the project interventions will 
deliver safe results as presumably they are designed to reduce loss of life and property in the 
event of catastrophic floods. This carries the risk of maladaptation. The iTAP recognizes the fact 
that the various scenarios track each other in terms of climate change impact in the medium 
term, and that the European Union (EU) – Bosnia and Herzegovina  has EU candidate status and 
seeks to achieve compliance with the region’s acquis communautaire – requires application of 
the RCP 8.5 scenario to comply with the EU Flood Directive; but recommends that the 
anticipated impact proposed project interventions be considered under alternative climate 
change scenarios by the AE prior to project commencement (recommendation).  
14. 
A major concern when evaluating the expected adaptation impacts of the proposed 
project relates to the lack of recent local stakeholder involvement in its design. Experience with 
similar projects strongly suggests that the success and sustainability of flood risk management 
approaches greatly depend on effective stakeholder consultation, and integration of different 
views from different groups into project design. While the AE has conducted some limited 
stakeholder consultations in 2018 and 2019 (21 bilateral meetings with individuals, including 
mayors, and representatives of civil protection departments and businesses), since then, no 
further consultations have been conducted. In the Q&A, the AE stated that between 2019 and 
2023 consultations with local stakeholders have continued via two UNDP-implemented projects 
(the GCF-funded “Advance the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) Process for Medium-term 
Investment Planning in Climate Sensitive sectors in B&H” and the Joint Swiss United Nations 
Development Programme “Disaster Risk Reduction for Sustainable Development in B&H”) but 
no summaries of these consultations have been made available. There is also no indication of 
how any feedback received may have been included in the design of the proposed project 
interventions. In the iTAP’s view, this presents a significant risk to the project. Thus, the iTAP 
recommends the Board adopt a condition as part of its approval of the funding proposal that 
would require the AE to conduct additional consultations covering the entire project region 
regarding the design of the project and how the local, entity, national and regional institutions 
may be involved, with a view towards future sustainability, in the production of the project’s 
results. The AE should submit a summary of the outcomes to the Secretariat (proposed 
condition 1, see below for more information). 
15. 
Likely the most significant challenge for implementation and thus the achievement of 
the anticipated adaptation impacts is related to the political and institutional complexity of the 
governance system in Bosnia and Herzegovina, driven (among other things) by i) ethnopolitical 
divisions (the country consists of two largely autonomous entities, Republika Srpska and the 
Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina, along with the Brčko District that are formed along 
ethnic lines, with separate government structures and decision-making processes); ii) 
complicated decision-making processes (the governance structure of Bosnia and Herzegovina

=== PAGE 149 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 149 
 
 
 
 
involves multiple layers of authorities, including a central government, entity governments, 
cantonal governments and municipal governments); iii) vetoes and blocking mechanisms 
(entities and ethnic groups have the power to veto decisions that go against their interests, 
resulting in a high level of political gridlock and difficulty in implementing necessary reforms); 
and iv) a lack of fiscal autonomy (the entities in Bosnia and Herzegovina are heavily reliant on 
financial transfers from the central government, which creates a financial imbalance and 
dependence). The funding proposal references these challenges, refers to UNDP’s experience in 
working successfully in this complicated environment and highlights the co-financing 
commitments that various Bosnia and Herzegovina entities have made as evidence that the 
project will have significant government buy-in and support. This highlights significant interest 
in the project and high likelihood of success in continued and formalized stakeholder 
engagement.  
16. 
In the iTAP’s view, however, the most effective mechanism to ensure that the project 
delivers sustainable results in this complex environment would be to implement the proposed 
project interventions in close partnership with the relevant entities and other institutions in 
Bosnia and Herzegovina on a day-to-day basis. The Direct Implementation Modality proposed 
by the AE, however, does not facilitate such extensive ownership of project implementation by 
relevant partners in the country (see also section 1.6 on country ownership). Indeed, the 
funding proposal makes only limited reference to how the AE will involve institutions of Bosnia 
and Herzegovina in the actual implementation of project interventions. The Project 
Management Unit will be hosted by the UNDP Country Office rather than a partner institution. 
(It is worthwhile noting in that context that the Project Implementation Unit supporting the 
implementation of the structural measures funded by the European Investment Bank (EIB) will 
be based at the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of Republika Srpska.) 
In the iTAP’s view, the concentration of execution, implementation and project assurance 
responsibilities within UNDP presents a material risk for the project and may undermine its 
potential to achieve the envisaged adaptation impacts in a sustainable manner. To mitigate this 
risk, the iTAP recommends the Board adopt a second condition (condition 2), to be fulfilled 
before second disbursement, consisting of development of a sustainability plan by the AE. The 
sustainability plan, building on the updated stakeholder consultations, should outline the 
various local/entity/national/regional institutions’ mandates and roles in the production and 
maintenance of project results (see below for more information).  
17. 
Finally, under output 3 the funding proposal suggests implementing activity 3.1: 
“Develop a country-wide investment framework for climate induced flood risk reduction and 
management including provisions for private sector engagement in climate risk financing”. The 
deliverables under this activity largely consist of feasibility studies, as well as the development 
of strategies and tools to be developed by consultants. It remains unclear which partners the AE 
would work with in the development of these deliverables, and how the AE will ensure that 
these deliverables will translate into concrete results. This is especially relevant when the 
project proposes to improve the enabling environment (for example, for products related to 
national disaster insurance) which presumably would require engagement with policymakers 
and relevant regulators and government agencies, but where specific information on how such 
engagement will be initiated is not included. The funding proposal also references various 
public-private partnerships being established and public–private sector climate risk financing 
mechanisms and tools developed, but it remains unclear what private sector entities would be 
involved (aside from the co-financier) and who would ultimately utilize the various products 
and tools developed. In its implementation plan and reporting to GCF, the AE should provide 
further clarification on these matters to ensure that the project does not just result in studies 
and tools that do not find practical application (recommendation).  
18. 
Overall, the iTAP rates the impact potential for the project as medium, with potential 
for improvement if the issues highlighted above are adequately addressed.

=== PAGE 150 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 150 
 
 
 
 
1.3 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
 
 
Scale: Medium 
19. 
The project aims to transform the current flood risk management in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina in response to climate change by bolstering institutional capabilities at all levels, 
enhancing comprehension of risk through improved climate information, incorporating non-
structural ecosystem-focused risk management methods, involving the private sector, and 
ensuring that both public and private investments in flood risk management are resilient to 
climate impacts. The project anticipates achieving a paradigm shift in flood risk management on 
five levels: 
(a) 
Introduction of technological innovations (implementation of fully integrated impact 
based Flood Forecasting and Early Warning System based on state-of-the-art 
observation, modelling, forecasting and communication platforms and technologies); 
(b) 
Capacity development and enhancement (capacity to identify, plan and implement long-
term flood risk management strategies at the basin and sub-basin scale); 
(c) 
Mainstreaming of nature-based flood risk management solutions in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina (encompassing EbA approaches as well as non-structural solutions); 
(d) 
Development of long-term climate risk financing (including via the development of 
municipal risk management plans); and 
(e) 
Introduction of innovative approaches to flood risk management (including forecast-
based financing approaches). 
20. 
The iTAP recognizes the project’s potential to substantially improve current flood risk 
management practices in Bosnia and Herzegovina. For a comprehensive paradigm shift to occur, 
however, the different project interventions will have to demonstrate sustainability over time. 
The various commitments that relevant  partners of Bosnia and Herzegovina have made with 
regard to, for example, financing long-term operation and maintenance (O&M) expenses for the 
hydrometric network equipment procured and installed via this project are a positive 
demonstration of commitment. Many of the co-financing commitments included in the funding 
proposal reviewed by the iTAP date back to 2019/2020, however, and should be renewed and 
resubmitted to GCF, prior to project commencement (recommendation).  
21. 
In the Q&A, the AE indicates that it will take a comprehensive approach to ensuring 
reliability of long-term O&M finance from different (public and private) sources, an approach 
that the iTAP believes is crucial. The funding proposal, however, lacks clarity in terms of how 
the project would concretely leverage different funding sources, and on what timeline. It 
remains unclear, for example, how the project’s proposed activity 3.1 would directly generate 
private-sector funding streams in support of O&M and other flood risk management/early 
warning system financing requirements, except with regard to exploring the option to introduce 
insurance products at conceptual level via the production of feasibility studies. The AE should 
clarify this as part of its regular reporting obligations to GCF (recommendation). 
22. 
Apart from the important commitments partners of Bosnia and Herzegovina have 
provided regarding long-term O&M finance, the funding proposal lacks sufficient detail on how 
the project will ensure that the various activities it undertakes and that will require sustained 
attention after project completion will be taken on by the relevant institutions within the 
country. The AE should generate a detailed sustainability/handover plan, specifying which 
institutions it will engage and work with to ensure “institutionalized operationalization” (e.g. 
how the institutions that ultimately will be responsible for the system will be involved in 
specific tasks, such as developing standard operating procedures) and continuity of the various 
project activities after project closure (condition 3, see below for more information). This plan 
should be completed and submitted to the Secretariat prior to the second disbursement. This 
should include all project activities. Thus, the plan to be submitted to the Secretariat should

=== PAGE 151 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 151 
 
 
 
 
make specific operational, maintenance and sustainability provisions for each activity to be 
funded by GCF. 
23. 
A significant portion of the funding that GCF would provide to the project would be in 
support of implementation of eight non-structural flood risk reduction measures (roughly USD 
6,74 million of the USD 14,4 million requested in total). The funding proposal highlights the 
reality that owing to the predominant use of engineered structural measures in mitigation of 
flood risk in Bosnia and Herzegovina historically, there is a lack of knowledge about non-
structural measures and limited capacity to implement them. The iTAP welcomes the fact that 
this project (building on the experience with the implementation baseline Vrbas project) 
intends to further mainstream the utilization of non-structural (i.e. zoning and land-use 
planning) as well as EbA approaches to flood risk management in Bosnia and Herzegovina. It 
remains unclear, however, from the funding proposal how that mainstreaming is supposed to be 
accomplished. The proposed budget for that subactivity will be utilized entirely for consulting 
fees, and it is unclear how the project will engage with relevant institutions within the country 
to ensure mainstreaming does take place and will be reflected in relevant investment plans, etc. 
These aspects should be addressed in the sustainability plan (condition 2). 
24. 
Overall, the iTAP rates the potential for the project to effect paradigm shift as medium, 
with potential for improvement if the issues highlighted above are adequately addressed and 
the various recommendations are implemented. 
1.4 
Sustainable development potential  
 
Scale: Medium-High 
25. 
Within the limits of the modelling exercise as discussed earlier, the funding proposal 
presents a credible pathway for the project to generate the following sustainable development 
benefits: 
(a) 
Environmental co-benefits: The funding proposal states that the project will generate 
environmental co-benefits largely via the implementation of EbA) strategies to flood risk 
management. Since the specific EbA measures to be implemented by the project have 
not yet been defined, it is difficult to conclude with certainty what (if any) specific 
environmental co-benefits will be generated. However, the iTAP concurs with the AE 
that the generic examples of EbA strategies highlighted in the funding proposal should 
in theory generate environmental co-benefits. 
(b) 
Economic co-benefits: The economic co-benefits expected from the project are a 
function of the losses avoided from floods that are predicted to occur based on the 
project’s flood risk modelling exercise. It should be noted that the socioeconomic 
modelling contained in the feasibility study is largely based on out-dated data since the 
study was originally constructed in 2018/2019. For example, the costs for the 
reconstruction of buildings following floods are based on data calculated by the Centre 
for Development Bills of Quantities for public buildings in 2015. In the Q&A, the AE 
provided some indicative information on how price levels have moved since completion 
of the feasibility study(e.g. based on Consumer Price Index data). Especially with the 
impact of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) (e.g. supply chain challenges) and 
inflation, the modelling may significantly underestimate potential economic losses from 
floods. Also, and as previously discussed, considering that the flood risk modelling and 
the intervention design of the project are based on the extreme RCP 8.5 scenario, the 
modelling generates avoided losses from expected extreme weather events, and thus the 
results need to be interpreted with some caution. The estimates for avoided losses on 
business assets is based on a damage function for the non-residential sector in the 
United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland. Bosnia and Herzegovina is an 
upper-middle income country, and  should thus have been compared with upper-middle 
and lower-middle income countries (e.g. Albania, Botswana). The AE clarified that at the

=== PAGE 152 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 152 
 
 
 
 
time the feasibility study was compiled, no data from comparable countries were 
available. The AE also noted that for the purposes of the damage calculation, only 
damages to buildings were taken into account. Overall, the iTAP concurs that if 
implemented successfully and sustainably, the project should generate considerable 
economic co-benefits for Bosnia and Herzegovina. 
(c) 
Social co-benefits: In the discussion of the Theory of Change (section B.2a) for the 
project, the funding proposal refers to potential health benefits that the project is 
expected to engender. The improved health and reduced health risks of the project’s 
beneficiaries are presented as a function of the reduction in the number of flood-related 
injuries and exposure to waterborne diseases. In general, the iTAP agrees that such 
health benefits are likely to manifest themselves if the project is successfully 
implemented and sustainable over time. However, no specific data on the project region 
is presented in the funding proposal to support this supposition so it is not possible to 
quantify these potential health benefits. It is also noteworthy that these potential social 
co-benefits are not discussed again in section D.3 (“Sustainable development”) of the 
funding proposal. 
(d) 
Gender-sensitive development co-benefits: The funding proposal includes an 
evidence-based gender assessment (annex 8), the results of which have been 
incorporated into the project’s Theory of Change and the design of the specific proposed 
project activities. For each activity, the funding proposal highlights gender 
mainstreaming activities. The iTAP welcomes this structured approach to incorporating 
analysis on existing inequalities into project design across all levels.  
(e) 
Overall, the iTAP rates the sustainable development impact potential of the project as 
medium–high. 
1.5 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
Scale: Medium-High 
26. 
Following the war (1992–1995), Bosnia and Herzegovina faced a severe economic crisis. 
The conflict had devastating effects on the country's economy, leading to the destruction of 
infrastructure, the collapse of industries, and the displacement of the country’s population. In 
this post-war situation, Bosnia and Herzegovina heavily relied on external assistance and aid to 
recover and rebuild. International organizations, such as UNDP and the World Bank, played a 
critical role in providing financial support. The EU has also been a significant donor and partner 
in the country’s post-war recovery. Through various financial programmes and assistance 
packages, the EU has supported the state’s economic development, governance reforms and 
integration processes. The stabilization and association agreement, signed with the EU in 2008, 
aimed to boost the country's economic and political stability. Remittances from Bosnian citizens 
living abroad have also played a crucial role in the country's post-war economy. Many Bosnians 
migrated to Western Europe and the United States of America during and after the war, and 
their remittances have provided a significant source of income for their families and 
communities back home.  
27. 
Bosnia and Herzegovina is an upper-middle income country and ranks 79th (out of 185) 
in the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative Country Index (with a vulnerability score of 
0.348 and a readiness score of 0.365). The index assesses the country’s vulnerabilities as 
manageable but improvements in readiness are advisable. As also emphasized in the funding 
proposal, and despite the external assistance and aid, the post-war economic situation in Bosnia 
and Herzegovina has been, and remains, challenging. The war deeply impacted the country's 
industrial base, leading to a decline in manufacturing and limited economic diversification. The 
complex political system and disputes between the two entities, Republika Srpska and the 
Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina, have contributed to a slow pace of structural reforms 
and hindered economic progress. The impacts of climate change are likely to further stress

=== PAGE 153 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 153 
 
 
 
 
Bosnia and Herzegovina’s economy, with increasing risk of floods and associated damage being 
just one manifestation of how climate change may impact the country negatively in the years 
and decades to come. From that standpoint, the assistance potentially made available by GCF for 
this project will make an important contribution to strengthening readiness and mitigate 
growing vulnerabilities to the impacts of climate change, specifically in the context of increasing 
flood risks.  
28. 
The AE did not include, as part of the funding proposal, an analysis of alternative 
sources of financing that might potentially be available. The iTAP enquired whether any 
assistance may be available from the EU, considering that Bosnia and Herzegovina is an 
accession candidate and does receive support from the regional block to achieve compliance 
with the EU’s acquis communautaire. Compliance with the EU Directive on the assessment and 
management of flood risks (which compels EU member States and candidate countries to apply 
the RCP 8.5 scenario in their flood risk modelling) is one example of how Bosnia and 
Herzegovina tries to solidify its status as a potential EU accession country. The AE responded 
that the EU already makes assistance available to the project via the EIB’s concessional 
financing for structural measures, and that no further (grant) support is available under the 
Instrument for Pre-accession Assistance.  
29. 
The reliance on external assistance and aid since the end of the Bosnian conflict has also 
created a level of dependency that the country must reduce to achieve sustainable economic 
growth. While the proposed project has the potential to significantly improve flood risk 
management practices in Bosnia and Herzegovina, the proposed Direct Implementation 
Modality carries the risk of further aggravating such dependency if the relevant entities and 
other institutions are not properly capacitated to maintain and further extend the “soft” and 
“hard” flood risk management infrastructure that is being funded by the project. As noted 
earlier, the current lack of specifics on how the AE will engage with relevant  institutions within 
the country during project implementation and the lack of a proper handover (or exit) plan is of 
concern to the iTAP. 
30. 
Overall, the iTAP rates the potential for the project to serve the needs of the recipients 
as medium–high. 
1.6 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
Scale: Medium-High 
31. 
Coherence with existing policies: The proposed project is well aligned with national 
climate change strategies and plans of Bosnia and Herzegovina. Specifically, the funding 
proposal responds to the country’s nationally determined contribution for 2020–2030 that 
specifically references the need for strengthening adaptative capacity in light of growing, 
climate change-induced flood risk. The funding proposal also refers to the Climate Change 
Adaptation and Low Emission Development Strategy of Bosnia and Herzegovina as adopted by 
the country’s Council of Ministers in 2013 (updated in 2020) that highlights increasing flood 
risks and lays out various adaptation priorities which align with the overall objectives of the 
funding proposal. Finally, the funding proposal dovetails with the NAP concluded in 2021 and 
that specifically references increasing flood risks (chapter 7) and the need for more proactive 
flood risk management in response to increasing climate change hazards.3 The iTAP would also 
like to highlight the signficant share of co-financing commitments received from entities and 
institutions in Bosnia and Herzegovina which signify buy-in and ownership (even though many 
of these commitments, as previously pointed out, are now dated and will have to be renewed). 
 
3 The NAP was developed with support from the GCF-funded “Advance the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) Process 
for Medium-term Investment Planning in Climate Sensitive sectors in B&H” (Readiness and Preparatory Support 
Project).

=== PAGE 154 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 154 
 
 
 
 
32. 
Capacity of the AE: UNDP has implemented USD 733 million worth of programmatic 
activities between 1996 and 2022 in Bosnia and Herzegovina, and thus has a long and extensive 
track record working in the country. Through the baseline project, as well as other related 
engagements (“Advance the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process for medium-term 
investment planning in climate sensitive sectors in B&H” (GCF Readiness and Preparatory 
Support Project) and “Scaling-up Investment in Low-Carbon Public Buildings”, co-financed by 
GCF (FP051)), UNDP has garnered significant experience working on adaptation issues within 
the country. 
33. 
Stakeholder consultations and engagement: As previously noted in section 1.2, the 
iTAP notes with concern the limited local stakeholder consultations that have been conducted 
for the preparation of this funding proposal that potentially undermines the project’s impact 
proposal, but also ownership (and thus long-term sustainability). The three proposed 
conditions provide a route to rectify this. 
34. 
The iTAP considers the alignment of the project with host country government priorities 
to be high but assesses the AE’s track record as medium. Overall, the iTAP rates country 
ownership of the funding proposal as medium–high. 
1.7 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
 
Scale: Medium 
35. 
The funding proposal highlights the following three aspects to demonstrate the 
economic soundness of the project: 
(a) 
A significant share of co-financing is from other public and private sector sources (GCF 
funding represents only 19.8 per cent of the estimated project cost); 
(b) 
The results of the economic analysis show an economic internal rate of return of 19.3 
per cent and thus demonstrate that the individual project interventions are 
economically efficient (with the exception of one); and 
(c) 
A cost of USD 80.50 per direct beneficiary for climate-resilient flood risk protection and 
early warning system support provided by the project which the AE deems as 
“comparable” to other projects of similar nature. 
36. 
Overall, the iTAP agrees that the proposed project performs satisfactorily with regard to 
the principles of economy and efficiency. However, the iTAP would like to make the following 
observations: 
(a) 
Regarding the first point, and as previously noted, the iTAP commends the large share of 
co-financing committed to this project from other sources, and particularly from Bosnia 
and Herzegovina’s partners. This signifies buy-in and ownership. The iTAP notes that 
the funding package for the project also includes a concessional loan from EIB with a 
volume of EUR 21.2 million (approximately USD 22.65 million at the exchange rate at 
time of writing), while the funding portion from GCF is expected to be made available in 
grant form. The AE notes that none of the interventions co-financed by GCF will result in 
any quantifiable earnings or savings for entities or local governments in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina, that they are predominantly focused on providing technical assistance, and 
that as a result of the current economic crisis (aggravated by the COVID-19 pandemic), 
the government of Bosnia and Herzegovina is not in a state to raise additional external 
debt. However, the iTAP would like to emphasize that i) Bosnia and Herzegovina (with a 
2023 gross domestic product per capita of EUR 7,366 (projection)) is considered an 
upper middle-income country by the World Bank; and ii) per the International Monetary 
Fund’s latest 2023 Article IV Consultation Staff Report,4 the country’s external debt is 
 
4 International Monetary Fund 2023. Bosnia and Herzegovina. 2023 Article IV Consultation – Press Release; Staff 
Report; and Statement by the Executive Director for Bosnia and Herzegovina. See

=== PAGE 155 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 155 
 
 
 
 
assessed to be sustainable over the medium-term. The external debt to gross domestic 
product ratio has been declining consistently over the past few years (to about 52 per 
cent in 2022). In that context, the International Monetary Fund states “[…] that here is 
room to increase IFI [international financial institution] financing to boost investment 
and growth potential”.5 Investments in adaptation to enhance the country’s capacity to 
avoid economic losses as a result of flood events should be considered important 
investments into the country’s growth potential, and as such it is not immediately 
obvious to the iTAP why GCF co-financing should be provided in the form of a grant, 
especially considering the pressing needs for adaptation and mitigation financing in 
least developed countries that GCF needs to respond to. 
(b) 
Regarding section 36 (c) above, the AE does not provide any cost data from comparable 
projects, and thus makes validation of its claim difficult. Since adaptation investments 
also tend to be highly bespoke to the specific country and regional context in which they 
are made, the iTAP is not clear how presentation of such average cost per beneficiary 
data provides a useful indication of the economic soundness of the project. 
1.8 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
37. 
The iTAP endorses this funding proposal, suggesting that the Board approve it subject to 
the three conditions specified below. 
38. 
Overall, the funding proposal is comprehensive and ambitious, and has potential to 
generate significant adaptation as well as broader sustainable development benefits for Bosnia 
and Herzegovina. The project, if successfully implemented by and with local, entity and national 
institutions, will fundamentally upgrade flood risk management in the country across various 
dimensions, with significant socioeconomic, environmental and social benefits for the target 
population in the project region, and for the population of Bosnia and Herzegovina as a whole.  
39. 
The iTAP welcomes the significant share of co-financing raised from other international 
and local funding sources which underscores the relevance of the project as well as the degree 
of ownership by key public and private sector stakeholders in the country. UNDP has 
considerable experience in planning and implementing adaptation projects in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina, and is thus well-positioned to lead the implementation of this project.  
40. 
In order to ensure that the project can unfold its full impact and paradigm shift 
potential, the iTAP recommends that the Board include the following condition to disbursement 
to its potential approval of this funding proposal: 
1.8.1. 
Stakeholder Consultation:

Entity, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF: 
(a) 
“Deliver to  the GCF Secretariat a summary report of stakeholder consultations 
conducted pursuant to the covenant below including recommendations for project 
design and implementation modalities.” 
42.

(a) 
“Prior to the first disbursement of GCF proceeds, the Accredited Entity shall conduct 
local stakeholder consultations across the entire project area, including in each of the 
target river basins regarding project design. The local consultations shall be conducted

Advisory Panel's assessment (FP216) 
Proposal name: 
Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina 
Accredited entity: 
United Nations Development Programme 
Country/(ies): 
Bosnia and Herzegovina 
Project/programme size:  
Medium 
 
Impact potential 
Paragraphs 12-13: UNDP acknowledges the iTAP’s observations regarding the methodology 
for the flood risk and vulnerability modelling and mapping that forms the basis of the 
calculation of the anticipated adaptation impacts.  
We are confident in the rationale behind the choice of scenario RCP8.5 for the estimation of 
impact of climate change-driven flooding in BiH, which is projected to result in increased 
frequency and intensity of flooding with the highest flood risk, highest flood levels and 
highest discharge of all CC scenarios. Large flood events such as expected under RCP8.5, are 
likely to result in substantial damage to infrastructure, agriculture, and communities.  The 
approach to adapting to these projections of future flooding risks is to invest in flood 
defenses, early warning systems, and sustainable land use planning.  
The modelled 100yr+CC (using RCP8.5) flood levels for Vrbas basin were found to be similar 
to the flood levels observed in the 2014 flood event. Given that the 2014 flood impacted all 
target basins and represented the worst flooding in each of these basins, it is reasonable to 
extrapolate the RCP8.5 flood impact modelled in Vrbas basin, to other basins. We are therefore 
confident that the use of RCP8.5 and its extrapolation to other basins, provides a robust climate 
change scenario for the assessment of flood risk (identification of population and assets at 
risk), the design of flood protection measures (structural, non-structural and NbS), and the 
impact of these adaptation measures. As noted by ITAP, this approach also follows the EU flood 
directive and was requested by stakeholders given they had already in the present day 
experienced what should have been an extreme flood under a future extreme scenario. We also 
note that since RCP8.5 gives the highest flood levels (of those tested) and that we expect it to 
produce the largest increases in rainfall intensity between the different RCP scenarios, the 
protection measures are expected to provide protection under both RCP6 and RCP4.5.    
Whilst flooding under moderate (RCP4.5) and intermediate (RCP6) climate change scenarios 
are likely to result in smaller magnitude, lower intensity events floods, they may occur more 
frequently and therefore (as noted by ITAP) still cause damage due to their shorter response 
time for authorities and communities to prepare, respond, and recover.  These types of events 
are likely to impact people and communities on an annual and seasonal basis and could be 
highly disruptive and economically costly. For these types of flood events, sustainable land use 
(through flood zoning, flood resilient agric. and forestry etc,), early warning systems (including 
CBEWS), preparedness (including FbF), insurance, investment planning (which will also factor 
in the economic impact and management of smaller more frequent floods), awareness raising 
and capacity building are most appropriate and are being implemented by the project. Their

=== PAGE 158 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 158 
 
 
 
design and implementation will, therefore, need to be fully informed by all relevant climate 
change scenarios to cover the full range of adaptation measures that will be required for 
different types of events.   
During year 1 and year 2 of implementation, the WBIF hazard maps for all basins will be 
enhanced and, in line with flood modelling and mapping best-practice, the impact on flooding 
of all relevant climate change scenarios will be simulated using individual calibrated hydraulic 
models for all basins, the results from which will be used to inform detailed design and 
implementation of flood risk reduction interventions. As recommended by ITAP, this will 
include using RCP4.5 for the hydrological simulations, but unfortunately RCP6.0 is not available 
for the EU-CORDEX regional climate models used to provide the climate forcing for the 
hydrological modelling. For the reasons noted above, RCP8.5 is likely to remain most relevant 
for the design and implementation of protection measures, while the lower impact RCP4.5 
scenario  will additionally inform other flood risk reduction measures.   
Paragraph 14: UNDP fully agrees that regular and effective stakeholder consultations and 
engagement are critically important for the success and sustainability of the flood risk 
reduction measures and for achieving the anticipated adaptation impact. This has been 
achieved through continuous consultations with the institutional stakeholders and local 
communities which have been ongoing throughout project preparation. Most recently, all 
institutional stakeholders (entity ministries of agriculture, forestry and water management, 
water agencies, civil protection units) were consulted through the GCF NDA in the process of 
securing the updated no-objection letter in June 2023. Consultations with the local 
communities to be targeted by this project were carried out during the project design and 
continued more recently in 2021-2023 via two UNDP implemented projects on the National 
Adaptation Plan (NAP) and on disaster risk reduction action.   
As noted above, UNDP agrees with the iTAP’s suggestions to reinforce stakeholder and 
community consultation processes, which are planned during the start of the project. The 
national and local stakeholder consultations will be carried out and reported during the 
project inception phase as part of the planned baseline assessments and then regularly 
during implementation through the stakeholder engagement mechanisms outlined in the 
stakeholder engagement plan. Please refer to our detailed feedback on the iTAP condition 1 in 
the Overall remarks Section. 
Paragraph 16: We are in agreement with iTAP highlighting that the sustainability of the 
project’s results and impacts in the complex institutional context requires implementation of 
the project interventions in close partnership with the relevant entities and institutions in 
Bosnia and Herzegovina. Please note, that unlike the EIB loan project, which operates only in

policy, regulatory, institutional, capacity building, structural and non-structural investments.

various institutions and workstreams.

the iTAP. Over the decade of operating in the country, the UNDP Country Office in B&H has

project’s implementation, which will apply to the GCF project as follows:

developing long-term O&M financing strategies. Often, a combination of several mechanisms 
may be necessary to ensure a stable and diverse funding base for the long-term sustainability 
of the national hydrometric observation network and forecasting and early warning system.

part of our regular reporting obligations to GCF.

Paragraph 23: UNDP noted with thanks that iTAP welcomed this project’s plans to 
mainstream the utilization of non-structural and EbA approaches to flood risk management in 
Bosnia and Herzegovina. Such mainstreaming will involve a coordinated effort among various 
institutions and government partners, all of which are key project partners. The EbA 
mainstreaming activity (Activity 2.3) will build upon the outcomes of several other activities 
which are interlinked. Therefore, the stated mechanisms of delivering Activity 2.3 (i.e. the use 
of consultancy services) is one part of an integrated approach to mainstreaming EbA into 
FRM and a culmination of other project activities. Please also note a high co-financing ratio 
for the activities related to the non-structural and EbA measures (79% government co-
finance to 21% GCF financing), which demonstrates the strong government commitment to 
embedding NbS into FRM.  GCF funds will be used to provide relevant technical expertise and 
inputs to create enabling conditions for the implementation of non-structural and EbA 
solutions and the mainstreaming of EbA into FRM policy and practice. The following is a 
general roadmap for achieving mainstreaming. 
• 
Policy Framework. The entity governments will take the lead in developing the 
policy framework that integrates EbA into flood risk management which may 
involve creating or amending relevant entity laws and regulations. Key 
ministries, all project board members, will collaborate to ensure a holistic 
approach to EbA in flood risk management and the mainstreaming into policy.  
• 
Raising Awareness and Capacity Building. Awareness campaigns, workshops, 
and training sessions to educate stakeholders about the benefits of non-
structural measures and EbA in enhancing climate resilience and reducing risks 
(Activity 2.3).  
• 
Case studies.  The implementation of 21 non-structural and 5 EbA measures 
(Activity 2.2) will provide the basis for the development of methodologies, and 
best practices guidelines which would form the technical basis for policies. In 
addition, the project will collaborate with research institutions to gather 
scientific data on ecosystems, flood risk, and climate change impacts and 
provide evidence-based research to support policy advocacy efforts, and with 
environmental NGOs which can provide valuable expertise in assessing the 
ecological aspects of EbA solutions and can advocate for stronger EbA policies 
and 
regulations 
and 
hold 
the 
government 
accountable 
for 
their 
implementation. 
• 
Quantify Benefits and Co-Benefits.  Using the socio-economic modelling tool to 
be developed, the project will quantify the multiple benefits of non-structural 
measures and EbA, including flood risk reduction, improved water quality, 
carbon sequestration, and enhanced ecosystem services.  Through the project-
implemented measures the project will provide evidence of the cost-
effectiveness of non-structural and EbA solutions (under Activity 2.2), based on 
economic analyses comparing NbS to traditional structural approaches and 
highlighting the potential for reducing maintenance and operational costs over 
time.  This can demonstrate to stakeholders how non-structural measures and 
EbA solution can help achieve broader sustainability goals. 
• 
Link to Local Development Goals.  Non-structural and EbA measures will be 
included in the investment planning of high-risk municipalities as part of their 
overall FRM investment planning frameworks (under Activity 3.2), and will 
ensure alignment with local development goals, such as improved water 
management, sustainable agriculture, enhanced biodiversity, and resilient 
infrastructure.

• 
Access to finance and Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs).  The project is

Paragraph 37: We concur with iTAP’s observations regarding the existing pressures on the 
B&H post-war economy described in the iTAP review for the Needs of the Recipient 
(paragraph 28). As we also demonstrated in the financial analysis (Annex III(c) to the full 
proposal), the existing economic risks and fiscal pressures further aggravated by COVID and 
the Ukraine-Russia conflict limit the ability of B&H to borrow. The shortfall in expenditure in 
B&H on climate change adaptation is due to the lack of financial capacity of the B&H 
Government to fund such activities without assistance.  
The GCF co-financing framework includes a significant co-financing from the Government 
including the EIB loan resources, which are utilized by the government to implement 
structural flood protection measures resulting in reduced damages and losses from floods. 
GCF co-financing will be used to create enabling environment, establish/improve risk 
knowledge and information baseline, mainstream innovative EbA solutions, and deliver 
capacity building required to achieve transformative shift in flood risk management in the 
country. The GCF support secures activities that enable and enhance the current and future 
floor risk reduction and protection interventions in the context of climate change. Therefore, 
we believe that the GCF grant resource are requested and justified for this intervention. 
None of the activities or outputs funded by GCF clearly result in direct and quantifiable 
earnings or direct and quantifiable savings to the project owners or the project beneficiaries. 
All the activities and outputs of this proposed project result only in non-attributable savings 
that are of public good in nature to the larger community of at-risk population in B&H. Hence, 
taking into consideration the factors such as the public good nature of benefits arising out of 
the project, climate change being the key driver, economic, geographic and demographic 
distribution of the target population, impact of COVID-19 and energy crisis, B&H’s current

=== PAGE 164 ===
GCF/B.37/02/Add.07 
Page 164 
 
 
 
economic situation, the persistent fiscal risks and the catalytic nature of GCF grants, the 
following has been concluded:  
1. There is significant co-financing from Government of B&H and there is no incremental 
ability to stretch their contributions owing to budgetary and fiscal consolidation 
reasons.  
2. The nature of the benefits does not accommodate repayment of capital in whatever 
form or serviceability of a loan instrument. 
3. The nature of the beneficiaries and the level of essentiality of the service do not 
accommodate repayment of capital in whatever form or serviceability of a loan 
instrument. 
4. Increase in government spending to address COVID-19 pandemic and energy crisis has 
strained public finances.

Secretariat together with the sustainability plan itself. We also suggest that this structure is

However, in relation to the equipment property rights please note that the transfer of assets is 
regulated by the SBAA process between UNDP and the Government. Clause 23.4 of the AMA 
between UNDP and the GCF stipulates the following in this respect: “In relation to a Funded 
Activity that is a grant financed in whole or in part with GCF Proceeds, if any part of such 
grant is used to purchase any durable assets or equipment used to implement the relevant 
Funded Activity (such as vehicles or office equipment), upon completion of the Funded 
Activity or termination of the relevant FAA in accordance with its terms, the Accredited Entity 
shall take such steps in relation to such assets or equipment which it reasonably deems in 
the best interest of the continued operation of the Funded Activity taking into consideration 
the objectives of the Fund and the terms of the applicable SBAA.” UNDP therefore believes 
that the sustainability plan, while providing a general framework for roles in project 
implementation, including policy mainstreaming and the operationalizing and sustaining of the 
flood forecasting and early warning systems, should not preempt the rights and obligations of 
UNDP under the SBAA.  Given that this is already fully covered under the proposed condition 
for third disbursement, UNDP cannot agree to the additional covenant proposed under this 
Condition.

Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan 
Green Climate Fund Full Proposal 
7. Gender documentation_FP216_UNDP_BiH_26 Sep 2023 
2 
I. 
Introduction 
 
The GCF project will address the increasing vulnerability of BiH communities and livelihoods, to 
intensified climate-induced hydro-meteorological flood-related disasters (pluvial, fluvial, and 
torrential). One of the root causes of increasing vulnerability and damages from floods is lack of 
quality climate data and flood forecasting and early warning system. As a result, the BiH 
authorities are unable to make qualified decisions and the affected population cannot effectively 
cope with the risk. Lack of climate information and risk knowledge increase the vulnerability and 
costs from flooding.  
The GCF project will build resilience of BiH communities and livelihoods to climate-induced 
floods by catalyzing a nation-wide shift from ad hoc, a posteriori response approaches to holistic 
a priori flood risk management and adaptation approaches based on enhanced climate 
information, risk knowledge, flood forecasting and early warning. 
The project will scale-up successful prototypes tested under the UNDP supported GEF financed 
project “Technology transfer for climate resilient flood management in Vrbas River Basin”. 
This Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan has been prepared for the submission to the 
Green Climate Fund with the proposal “Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in BiH”. 
The assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in Bosnia and Herzegovina, 
identify gender issues that may be relevant to the project context, to develop recommendations 
for advancing gender equality and women’s empowerment through the financed activities, and 
to inform the development of the project’s gender action plan. Individual needs and contexts in a 
disaster situation are examined through the lenses of vulnerability, visibility, and voices. The 
analysis follows social vulnerability approach and further defines whose voice is seen as the 
default one, and whose is the most visible perspective. The assessment was based upon available 
data from the Agency for Statistics of Bosnia and Herzegovina, RS and FBiH, studies conducted by 
the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina, international organizations, civil society 
organizations and any other sources as UNDP Technology transfer for climate resilient flood 
management in Vrbas River Basin project and includes: 
1. 
Undertaking a desktop review and aligning approaches in this proposal with the 
national priorities of Bosnia and Herzegovina. 
2. 
Incorporating information and lessons learned from past studies and assessments 
on gender in Bosnia and Herzegovina from the Government of Bosnia and 
Herzegovina, the United Nations, civil society organisations, and multilateral 
development banks.  
3. 
Conducting stakeholder consultations, including consultations with the Agency 
for Gender Equality of Bosnia and Herzegovina and the two gender centers (of 
Republika Srpska and Federation BiH), and engaging women/men/youth affected 
by the project and incorporating all points raised; and 
4. 
Integrating gender considerations in the project indicators, targets, and activities, 
identifying women as leaders and decision-makers 
 While it is noted that the Gender Analysis and Action Plan should specifically identify and analyze 
the most critical issues relevant to the project and whether the needs and priorities of an equal

=== PAGE 169 ===
I 
Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan 
Green Climate Fund Full Proposal 
7. Gender documentation_FP216_UNDP_BiH_26 Sep 2023 
3 
number of women/girls/men/boys are being assessed, this Gender Analysis and Action Plan is 
based on available published and grey literature, consultation with civil society organizations 
participated in recovery project after the floods happened in 2014 in Bosnia and Herzegovina and 
Gender Agency of Bosna and Herzegovina. Further an institutional gender analysis has been 
undertaken (although there will be need for deeper analysis and close cooperation during the 
project implementation). During project implementation, further stakeholder consultation will 
be undertaken to assess the components in relation to gender, age, and other relevant 
intersecting social and economic strata. There may be a need for the Gender Analysis and Action 
Plan to be modified depending on stakeholder consultation during the implementation of the 
project. 
UNDP prioritizes gender mainstreaming as its main strategy to achieve gender equality and 
women’s empowerment. Gender mainstreaming is the process of assessing any planned action in 
all areas and levels to determine the implication for women and men. It is a strategy for making 
women’s, as well as men’s, concerns and experiences an integral dimension of the design, 
implementation, monitoring and evaluation of projects so that women benefit equally. Gender 
mainstreaming aims to transform unequal social and institutional structures in order to make 
them profoundly responsive to gender, and, when realized, it ensures that both women and men 
benefit equally from the development process. It involves much more than simply adding 
women’s participation to existing strategies and programmes. Special attention and action are 
often required to compensate for the existing gaps and inequalities that women currently face.  
The UNDP Gender Equality Strategy 2022-2025 is aligned with the 2030 Development Agenda 
and UNDP’s Strategic Plan.  The strategy recognizes gender equality as a human right as well as 
instrumental to the achievement of sustainable development.  It considers women and men as 
active agents of change and development, not simply beneficiaries and vulnerable groups and it 
recognizes how working with men and boys is of critical importance to change gender norms and 
attitudes and achieve gender equality.  The gender assessment and action plan developed for the 
submission to the Green Climate Fund with the proposal “Scaling up climate resilient flood risk 
management in BiH” has been prepared to meet the requirements and standards in UNDP’s 
Gender Equality Strategy which meets or exceeds the requirements set forth by the Green Climate 
Fund Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan.   
Short background on Bosnia and Herzegovina and on EU Accession Process 
Bosnia and Herzegovina is a potential candidate for EU membership. The Stabilization and 
Association Agreement between Bosnia and Herzegovina and the EU was ratified in 2011.  In 
February 2016, the country applied for EU membership and in September 2016 the European 
Council invited the European Commission to submit its Opinion on the merits of Bosnia and 
Herzegovina's application. In December 2016, the Commissioner for European Neighborhood 
Policy and Enlargement Negotiations handed over a comprehensive Questionnaire covering all 
EU accession criteria. The country's consolidated answers were finalized in February 2018 and 
the answers handed over to the President of the European Commission. The Commission has 
prepared its “Opinion” report in 2019, based on the country's answers to the Questionnaire and 
follow up inquiries, dedicated peer reviews as well as Commission's consultations with 
international organizations and civil society. This interim report on Bosnia and Herzegovina 
provides an update on the situation in the country as well as on key developments on

=== PAGE 170 ===
I 
Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan 
Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

4 
"fundamentals' first" areas: the rule of law and fundamental rights, public administration reform 
and economic development. According to the Opinion of EU the legislation and institutions on 
fundamental rights are largely in place; they should be fully implemented and substantially 
improved, including by harmonizing legislation within the country and aligning it with European 
standards, strengthening administrative capacity and providing adequate resources for the 
effective enforcement of fundamental rights. There are no countrywide strategies on human 
rights and on antidiscrimination. The equality of all citizens is not ensured. The laws on non-
discrimination and on gender equality are in place but not sufficiently enforced, including on 
gender-based violence. Bosnia and Herzegovina does not have any policy areas where the country 
has a good level of preparation or is well advanced regarding its capacity to undertake the 
obligations stemming from EU membership. Overall, considerable adjustments of the legal and 
institutional framework and of administrative and implementation capacity are needed across all 
policy areas to align the legislation with the EU acquis and to implement it effectively. 1Bosnia and 
Herzegovina continued to implement the Stabilization and Association Agreement and the 
meetings of the joint bodies under the agreement took place, except for the parliamentary 
committee which has yet to adopt its Rules of Procedures2.  
 
 
Overview on Bosnia and Herzegovina Government Construction 
 
The current Constitution of Bosnia and Herzegovina came into existence in 1995 as an Annex to 
the General Framework Agreement for Peace in Bosnia and Herzegovina. While the General 
Framework Agreement for Peace in BiH ended the war and brought peace and stability, the extent 
to which the constitutional arrangement defined by its provisions is adequate under present 
circumstances, is widely debatable. According to the provisions of the Peace Agreement the State 
of Bosnia and Herzegovina is organized as a country composed of two entities with a broad set of 
competencies: the Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina (FBiH) and Republika Srpska (RS). Some 
years after, an arbitration decision on the disputed areas of the country made Brčko District the 
third administrative unit of BiH. RS is a centralized entity whereas the FBiH is comprised of ten 
Cantons. 
 
The State level comprises a tripartite rotating Presidency, a Council of Ministers (executive 
branch) and a bicameral Parliamentary Assembly consisting of a House of Representatives (lower 
chamber) and a House of Peoples (upper chamber). The judicial branch established by Dayton 
consists of a State-level Constitutional Court. The High Judicial and Prosecutorial Council, as well 
as a State Court and Prosecutor Office, were established later.  
 
An international presence under UN auspices— the Office of the High Representative (OHR) — 
has been in place in Bosnia and Herzegovina since 1995.  The Peace Implementation Council 
Steering Board has set objectives and conditions to be met for the closure of the OHR3. Since the 
decoupling of the European Union Special Representative’s mandate from the OHR, the EU has 
reinforced its role in the country in line with the relevant Council Conclusions. The enhanced

presence of the combined EU Special Representative and the EU Delegation in Bosnia and

II. 
Gender Equality/Inequality in Bosnia and Herzegovina  
Before starting to analyze gender inequality in Bosnia and Herzegovina, it is important to make 
clear and to agree on the meaning of gender equality.  
 
Gender equality refers to the equal rights, responsibilities and opportunities of women and men 
and girls and boys. Equality does not mean that women and men will become the same but that 
women’s and men’s rights, responsibilities and opportunities will not depend on whether they 
are born male or female. Gender equality implies that the interests, needs and priorities of both 
women and men are taken into consideration, recognizing the diversity of different groups of 
women and men. Gender equality is not a women’s issue but should concern and fully engage men 
as well as women. Equality between women and men is seen both as a human rights issue and as 
a precondition for, and indicator of, sustainable people-centered development (UN Women, OSAGI 
Gender Mainstreaming - Concepts and definitions) 
 
The fundamental principle of human development is for people to enlarge their choices, to realize 
their potential, and to enjoy the freedom to lead lives they value or have reason to value. Equal 
opportunities in all spheres, for all people, women, and men alike, are at the heart of the human 
development. However, those options remain unequally distributed within and across societies 
and unfortunately many are still largely unavailable to women. Disadvantage and discrimination, 
lack of insights into what particularly affects women’s lives, including health (physical and 
emotional), reinforced by social norms and values, institutions, and public policies, exist in every 
sphere of women’s lives, manifested differently in different country settings. They limit women’s 
capabilities, opportunities, and choices, resulting in less progress than men in terms of health, 
education, standard of living, empowerment, personal security, and other critical dimensions of 
human development. Thus, the full potential of many women’s lives cannot reach optimal level 
and humanity as a whole does not reap its potentials (Issue papers, “Gender Equality in Human 
Development – Measurement Revisited”, Human Development Report Office, UNDP June 2015). 
 
According to the latest UNDP Human Development Report (2020), Bosnia and Herzegovina’s HDI 
value for 2019 is 0.780— which ranks it at 73 out of 189 countries and territories. Between 2000 
and 2019, Bosnia and Herzegovina’s HDI value increased from 0.679 to 0.780, an increase of 14.9 
percent. Bosnia and Herzegovina’s progress in each of the HDI indicators shows that between 
1990 and 2019, Bosnia and Herzegovina’s life expectancy at birth increased by 6.5 years, mean 
years of schooling increased by 2.8 years and expected years of schooling increased by 2.2 years. 
Bosnia and Herzegovina’s GNI per capita increased by about 737.8 percent between 1990 and 
2019. Bosnia and Herzegovina’s 2019 HDI of 0.780 is above the average of 0.753 for countries in 
the high human development group and below the average of 0.791 for countries in Europe and 
Central Asia.  The inequality HDI (IHDI) introduced in 2010, is basically the HDI discounted for 
inequalities. The ‘loss’ in human development due to inequality is given by the difference between 
the HDI and the IHDI and can be expressed as a percentage.  When discounted for inequality, 
Bosnia and Herzegovina’s HDI falls to 0.667, a loss of 14.5 percent due to inequality in the

=== PAGE 172 ===
I 
Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan 
Green Climate Fund Full Proposal 
7. Gender documentation_FP216_UNDP_BiH_26 Sep 2023 
6 
distribution of the HDI dimension indices. This is above the average loss due to inequality for high 
HDI for Europe and Central Asia which is 11.9 percent. 
 
With regard to Bosnia and Herzegovina, there can be no dispute that significant advances have 
been made in the realization of the principle of the equality of women and men, which is a result 
of activities taken by all parties (Gender institutional mechanisms, as well as citizens through civil 
society organizations) responsible for implementing the Law on Gender Equality in Bosnia and 
Herzegovina. Nonetheless, in spite of these positive changes, there are still a lot to be done.   
Through the years, several indices have developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality.  
The United Nations Development Programme uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Gender 
Development Index (GDI).4 GII is a composite measure that shows inequality in achievement 
between women and men in reproductive health, empowerment, and the labour market while 
with a measures achievement in human development in three areas: health, education, and 
command over economic resources.  The GDI considers the gender gaps on human development 
between men and women. 
The Gender Development Index (GDI) based on the sex-disaggregated Human Development 
Index, defined as a ratio of the female to the male HDI is 0.937 for Bosnia and Herzegovina (female 
HDI = 0.753 and male HDI = 0.803).  This is below the average for Europe and Central Asia, which 
is 0.953, and placing it in Group 3 – which comprises countries with medium equality in HDI 
achievements between women and men. The Gender Inequality Index (GII) reflects gender-based 
inequalities in three dimensions – reproductive health, empowerment, and economic activity. 
Reproductive health is measured by maternal mortality and adolescent birth rates; 
empowerment is measured by the share of parliamentary seats held by women and attainment 
in secondary and higher education by each gender; and economic activity is measured by the 
labour market participation rate for women and men. The GII can be interpreted as the loss in 
human development due to inequality between female and male achievements in the three GII 
dimensions.  Bosnia and Herzegovina has a GII value of 0.149, ranking it 38 out of 162 countries 
in the 2019 index. In Bosnia and Herzegovina, 21.1 percent of parliamentary seats are held by 
women, and 74.0 percent of adult women have reached at least a secondary level of education 
compared to 89.3 percent of their male counterparts. For every 100,000 live births, 10.0 women 
die from pregnancy related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 9.6 births per 1,000 women 
of ages 15-19. Female participation in the labour market is 35.4 percent compared to 58.1 for 
men.   
The Multi-dimensional Poverty Index (MPI), which identifies multiple overlapping deprivations 
suffered by individuals in 3 dimensions: health, education, and standard of living.  In Bosnia and 
Herzegovina, 2.2 percent of the population (73 thousand people) are multidimensionally poor 
while an additional 4.1 percent are classified as vulnerable to multidimensional poverty (135 
thousand people). The breadth of deprivation (intensity) in Bosnia and Herzegovina, which is the 
average deprivation score experienced by people in multidimensional poverty, is 37.9 percent. 
The MPI, which is the share of the population that is multidimensionally poor, adjusted by the 
intensity of the deprivations, is 0.008.  Compared to Income Poverty, the multidimensional

The Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) developed the Social 
Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), a composite index that scores countries (i.e., 0 to 1) on 14 
indicators grouped into five sub-indices: discriminatory family code, restricted physical integrity, 
son bias, restricted resources and assets, and restricted civil liberties to measure the 
discrimination against women in social institutions across 160 countries. The 2018 SIGI value for 
Bosnia and Herzegovina is 22%, suggesting that discrimination against women is low.6  
Political participation and Public Life 
 
According to the 2013 Law on Amendments to the Election Law of Bosnia and Herzegovina, equal 
representation of genders exists when one of the genders is represented by at least 40% of the 
total number of candidates on that list (previously, the quota was 33%).  Nowadays, the 
percentage of women in legislative and executive authorities at all levels remains unsatisfactory 
(even the situation is better comparing 2014 and 2018 elections) and the newly introduced 40% 
representation quota has not yet been reached. 
 
The last General Elections were held in 2018 in Bosnia and Herzegovina (State, entities, cantonal 
level, and Brčko District level). The Governments are still not fully established, but the available 
figures give an overview of the gender reality in B&H. There are 9 (21.4%) female representatives 
in the House of Representatives of the Parliamentary Assembly of Bosnia and Herzegovina out of 
42 members7 and 3 (20%) female delegates in the House of Peoples of the Parliamentary
